\clearpage
\section*{SGA 7-I, Introduction and Exposé I}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{SGA 7-I, Introduction and Exposé I}

\section*{Introduction}

Let $S$ be a scheme and let $f:X\to S$ be a morphism of schemes. If $f$ is proper and smooth, and the prime number $\ell$ is invertible on $S$, then the $\ell$-adic cohomology groups
\[
H^i(X_{\bar s},\ZZ_\ell)
\]
of the geometric fibres of $f$ form a locally constant sheaf on $S$. The problem considered here is to understand what remains of this statement when the smoothness hypothesis fails at a special fibre, and in particular to describe the specialization morphism, vanishing cycles, and monodromy.

We shall only consider the case where $S$ is a henselian trait, that is, the spectrum of a henselian discrete valuation ring. This is in practice the essential case. Let
\[
S=\Spec(V),
\]
with generic point $\eta$ and closed point $s$. The fundamental exact sequence is
\[
\cdots \longto H^i(X_s,\ZZ_\ell)
\xrightarrow{\mathrm{sp}}
H^i(X_{\bar\eta},\ZZ_\ell)
\longto R^i\Phi\,\ZZ_\ell
\longto H^{i+1}(X_s,\ZZ_\ell)
\longto\cdots .
\]
The $\Gal(\bar s/s)$-module $H^i(X_s,\ZZ_\ell)$ is regarded as a $\Gal(\bar\eta/\eta)$-module with trivial inertia action, and $\mathrm{sp}$ is the specialization map.

Criteria are also given, at present only in characteristic zero, for the vanishing of the small-degree sheaves $R^i\Phi$. For example, if the special fibre is locally a complete intersection, then
\[
R^i\Phi=0\qquad (i\neq n)
\]
in the isolated-singularity situation of I, 4.5.

The monodromy theorem asserts that the action of the inertia subgroup $I$ of $\Gal(\bar\eta/\eta)$ is quasi-unipotent: for $T\in I$ there exist integers $N>0$ and $M>0$ such that
\[
(T^N-1)^M=0
\quad\text{on}\quad
H^i(X_{\bar\eta},\ZZ_\ell).
\]
Applied to the $H^1$ of abelian varieties, i.e. to their Tate modules, these results allow one to study reduction modulo $p$. Two proofs are given of the stable reduction theorem for abelian varieties: after a finite extension of the base trait, the neutral component of the special fibre of the Néron model is an extension of an abelian variety by a torus.

\begin{flushright}
Bures-sur-Yvette, May 1972\\
P. Deligne
\end{flushright}

\section*{Exposé I -- Summary of the first exposés of A. Grothendieck}

\subsection*{Contents}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.]
\item Arithmetic proof of the monodromy theorem.
\item Vanishing cycles.
\item Geometric proof of the monodromy theorem.
\item Vanishing criteria for sheaves of vanishing cycles.
\item Action of monodromy on fundamental groups.
\item Appendix by P. Deligne: arithmetic proof of the stable reduction theorem.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{0. Preliminaries}

\subsubsection*{Terminology}

We recall the following definitions.

A \emph{trait} is the spectrum of a discrete valuation ring. Its generic and closed points are often denoted by $\eta$ and $s$.

An \emph{strictly local} ring is a henselian local ring with separably closed residue field. A strictly local scheme is the spectrum of such a ring.

A \emph{geometric point of $S$}, in this exposé, is a morphism
\[
\bar x:\Spec(k)\to S
\]
with image $x\in S$, where $k$ contains a separable closure of $k(x)$. For a henselian trait $S=\Spec(V)$, one often writes $\bar\eta$ for a geometric point over the generic point and $\bar s$ for one over the closed point.

The \emph{strict localization} of $X$ at a geometric point $\bar x$ is understood in the sense of SGA 4, VIII, 4.4.

A local, henselian local, or strictly local $S$-scheme is said to be \emph{essentially of finite type over $S$} if it is the spectrum of a local ring, respectively the henselization of such a local ring, respectively the strict localization, of a scheme of finite type over $S$.

\subsubsection*{Reminders on $\ell$-adic cohomology}

Let $\ell$ be a prime invertible on the schemes considered. We use the usual notation
\[
H^i(X_{\bar x},\ZZ_\ell)=\varprojlim_n H^i(X_{\bar x},\ZZ/\ell^n\ZZ).
\]
The functors $R^i\Psi$ and $R^i\Phi$ denote, respectively, nearby cycles and vanishing cycles. For $S$ a henselian trait and $X\to S$, the stalks of $R^i\Psi$ at a geometric point $\bar x$ of the special fibre are computed on the strict localization of $X$ at $\bar x$ after passage to the geometric generic fibre. The exact sequence displayed in the introduction is the long exact sequence associated with the specialization triangle.

\subsubsection*{Reminders on traits}

Let $S$ be a henselian trait with generic point $\eta$ and closed point $s$. The Galois group $\Gal(\bar\eta/\eta)$ has inertia subgroup $I$, and the quotient is $\Gal(\bar s/s)$. Its wild inertia subgroup is denoted by $P$. The tame quotient is
\[
I/P\simeq \prod_{\ell'\neq p}\ZZ_{\ell'}(1),
\]
where $p$ is the residual characteristic exponent.

\subsubsection*{The Néron desingularization}

A standard ingredient is Néron's desingularization. In the form used here, it permits one, after replacing a morphism by an étale neighbourhood or by a suitable model, to reduce many local questions to the case of regular schemes, or to schemes with normal crossings in the special fibre.

\paragraph{Proposition 0.1.}
Let $S$ be a trait and let $X$ be of finite type over $S$. After replacing $X$ locally by a suitable modification which is an isomorphism on the generic fibre, one may arrange that the singularities relevant for the vanishing-cycle calculation occur on a controlled normal-crossings model, whenever resolution of singularities is available.

\paragraph{Proposition 0.2.}
In equal characteristic zero, or under the corresponding resolution hypotheses, one can reduce the monodromy calculation to the case where the special fibre is a divisor with normal crossings.

\subsection*{1. Arithmetic proof of the monodromy theorem}

\paragraph{Proposition 1.1.}
Let $K$ be a complete discretely valued field and let $V$ be a finite-dimensional $\QQ_\ell$-vector space with continuous action of $\Gal(\bar K/K)$, arising from the cohomology of a scheme of finite type over $K$. After replacing $K$ by a finite extension, the action of inertia on $V$ is unipotent.

\paragraph{Theorem 1.2 (Monodromy theorem).}
Let $X$ be proper and smooth over the generic point $\eta$ of a henselian trait $S$, and let $\ell$ be invertible on $S$. For every $i$, the action of inertia on
\[
H^i(X_{\bar\eta},\QQ_\ell)
\]
is quasi-unipotent. Equivalently, for $T$ in an open subgroup of inertia,
\[
(T-1)^M=0
\]
for some $M$ depending on $i$.

The arithmetic proof proceeds by reducing to representations arising from the cohomology of varieties over finite fields and using the constraints imposed by weights. The unipotent part of the inertia action is then detected by comparing the possible weights of the graded pieces.

\subsection*{2. Vanishing cycles}

Let $S$ be a strictly local trait and let $f:X\to S$ be of finite type. The sheaves $R^i\Psi$ of nearby cycles and $R^i\Phi$ of vanishing cycles on the special fibre measure the failure of specialization
\[
H^i(X_s)\to H^i(X_{\bar\eta})
\]
to be an isomorphism.

For a geometric point $\bar x$ of $X_s$, $R^i\Psi_{\bar x}$ is the cohomology of the geometric generic fibre of the strict localization of $X$ at $\bar x$, and $R^i\Phi_{\bar x}$ is obtained from the cone of the natural map from the special fibre to the nearby fibre.

\paragraph{Proposition 2.1.}
If $f$ is smooth in a neighbourhood of $\bar x$, then
\[
R^0\Psi_{\bar x}\simeq\ZZ_\ell,\qquad R^i\Psi_{\bar x}=0\quad(i>0),
\]
and $R^i\Phi_{\bar x}=0$ for all $i$.

\paragraph{Proposition 2.2.}
The sheaves $R^i\Psi$ and $R^i\Phi$ commute with étale localization on $X$ and with strict localization at geometric points of the special fibre.

\paragraph{Proposition 2.3.}
If $X$ is replaced by the schematic closure of its generic fibre, then $R^i\Psi$ and $R^i\Phi$ are unchanged away from the nilpotent and vertical excess components. This often reduces the study to the flat case.

\subsection*{3. Geometric proof of the monodromy theorem}

\subsubsection*{Purity conjecture}

Let $X$ be regular, $D$ a divisor with normal crossings, and $U=X-D$. The purity statement needed here says that the tame fundamental group of $U$ near a point of $D$ is controlled by the local branches of $D$. Equivalently, the cohomology of the corresponding tame coverings has the expected exterior-algebra form.

Suppose that, étale locally at a point of the special fibre, the divisor is
\[
X_s=\sum_{i\in B}m_iD_i
\]
with normal crossings. Let $\bar x$ be a geometric point of $X$ localized at $x$, let
\[
C=\{\,i\mid x\in D_i\,\},
\]
and let $K$ be the complex concentrated in degrees $0$ and $-1$
\[
K:\bigoplus_{i\in C}\ZZ(1)\xrightarrow{\ d\ }\ZZ(1),
\]
where $d=\sum a_i$.

\paragraph{Theorem 3.3.}
Assuming purity, the tame vanishing-cycle groups $R^i\Psi_{\bar x}$ are described as follows:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\roman*)]
\item There is a canonical isomorphism of graded $A$-algebras
\[
R^*\Psi_{\bar x}\simeq H^*(K\otimes A)\otimes A[t],
\]
where $t$ has degree $2$.
\item The inertia action on $R^1\Psi_{\bar x}$ is described by a transitive action on a finite set whose cardinality is the prime-to-$p$ part of
\[
m=\gcd(m_i).
\]
\end{enumerate}

The proof proceeds as follows. Let $X_{\bar x}$ be the strict localization of $X$ at $\bar x$, and let
\[
U=X_{\bar x}-(X_s\cap X_{\bar x})
\]
be the complement of the normal-crossings divisor. If $t_i$ is a local equation of $D_i$, define for $(n,p)=1$
\[
U_n=\Spec\bigl(\cO_{X_{\bar x}}[T]/(T^n-\prod t_i^{a_i})\bigr),
\]
and let $\widetilde U_n$ be the inverse image of $U$ in $U_n$. Purity gives the cohomology of these covers. Passing to the projective limit over the tame coverings gives a pro-covering of $U$ with group
\[
\ZZ(1)(\overline{k(s)})^C.
\]
By definition,
\[
R^i\Psi_{\bar x}=H^i(\widetilde U,A).
\]
The connected components are permuted transitively by tame inertia, and the cohomology of each component is computed from the complex $K$.

\paragraph{Corollary 3.4.}
Assume purity and suppose $f$ proper. Let $N$ be the least common multiple of the multiplicities $m_j$. Let $q\geq0$, and let $q'$ be the minimum of $q$ and the maximum number of components $D_j$ passing through a single point. For $T$ in tame inertia $I/P$, one has
\[
(T^N-1)^{q'+1}=0
\quad\text{on}\quad
R^q\Psi_{\bar x}.
\]

\paragraph{Theorem 3.5.}
Let $C$ be a projective smooth curve over the fraction field of a discrete valuation ring $V$. The inertia group acts on
\[
H^1(C_{\bar\eta},\QQ_\ell)
\]
by quasi-unipotent automorphisms of level $2$: there exists $N$ such that, for $T\in I$,
\[
(T^N-1)^2=0.
\]

\paragraph{Remark 3.6.}
If $A$ is an abelian variety over $k(\eta)$, then, since $A$ is a quotient of a Jacobian, the same conclusion holds for the inertia action on $H^1(A_{\bar\eta},\QQ_\ell)$, or on the Tate module $T_\ell(A)$.

\paragraph{Remark 3.7.}
For every scheme $X$ of finite type over $\eta$, the action of inertia on
\[
H^i(X_{\bar\eta},\QQ_\ell)
\]
is in fact quasi-unipotent of level $2$.

When purity and resolution of singularities are available, for example in characteristic zero, the preceding method proves the monodromy theorem and gives bounds for the nilpotence exponent. In equal characteristic zero, for $X$ proper and smooth over $\eta$ and $T$ in an open subgroup of $I$, one finds
\[
(T-1)^{i+1}=0
\quad\text{on}\quad
H^i(X_{\bar\eta},\ZZ_\ell).
\]

\subsection*{4. Vanishing criteria for sheaves of vanishing cycles}

Let $S$ be a strictly local trait and let $f:X\to S$ be of finite type. Set
\[
n=\dim(X_\eta).
\]
If $X'$ is the schematic closure of $X_\eta$ in $X$, then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\alph*)]
\item $R^0\Phi\,\cO_{X'}=\cO_{X_s}\otimes\ZZ_\ell$ and the other $R^i\Phi$ vanish;
\item on $X'$, the $R^i\Phi$ and $R^i\Psi$ associated with $X/S$ and $X'/S$ agree.
\end{enumerate}
Since $X'$ is flat over $S$, this often reduces the problem to the flat case.

\paragraph{Theorem 4.2.}
One has
\[
R^i\Phi=0\qquad (i>n).
\]
More precisely, if $x$ is a point of $X_s$ whose closure has dimension $k$, and if $\bar x$ is a geometric point localized at $x$, then
\[
R^i\Phi_{\bar x}=0\qquad (i>n-k).
\]

\paragraph{Corollary 4.3.}
If $f$ is proper and flat and the non-smooth locus of $f$ in $X_s$ has dimension $<d$, then the specialization morphism
\[
H^i(X_s)\longto H^i(X_{\bar\eta})
\]
is an isomorphism for $i>n+d+1$ and an epimorphism for $i=n+d+1$.

To prove, in some cases, that the small-degree $R^i\Phi$ vanish, one uses local duality, either directly or through the local Lefschetz theorems of SGA 2, XIV. At present this tool is available in characteristic zero, or in equal characteristic $p$ when resolution of singularities in the relevant dimensions is available.

\paragraph{Theorem 4.5.}
Assume that $S$ has characteristic zero. Let $f:X\to S$ be of finite type, and let $x$ be a closed point of $X_s$. Suppose:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $x$ is an isolated non-smooth point in its fibre;
\item $X$ has relative geometric depth at least $n$ at $x$: in a neighbourhood of $x$, the scheme $X$ is defined by $k$ equations in a smooth scheme of relative dimension $N$ at $x$, with
\[
n<N-k.
\]
\end{enumerate}
Then
\[
R^i\Phi_{\bar x}=0\qquad (i<n).
\]

\paragraph{Corollary 4.6.}
Let $S$ be a henselian trait of characteristic zero, let $X/S$ be a relative complete intersection of relative dimension $n$, and let $x\in X_s$ be an isolated non-smooth point in its fibre. Then
\[
R^i\Phi_{\bar x}=0\qquad (i\neq n).
\]

\paragraph{Corollary 4.7.}
Under the hypotheses of 4.6, $R^n\Phi$ is a flat $\ZZ_\ell$-module.

\paragraph{Variant 4.8.}
In Theorem 4.5, omit condition (a), and let $Z$ be the non-smooth locus of $f$. Then one still obtains
\[
R^i\Phi=0\qquad (i<n-\dim Z).
\]

\paragraph{Proposition 4.10.}
Under the general hypotheses of 4.5, suppose:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $x$ is an isolated non-smooth point in its fibre;
\item for every point $y$ of $X_{\bar\eta}$, with closure of dimension $d(y)$ and specializing to $x$, one has
\[
\prof_{\mathrm{et},y}(X_{\bar\eta})\geq n-d(y);
\]
\item $\prof_{\mathrm{et},x}(X_s)\geq n$.
\end{enumerate}
Then
\[
R^i\Phi_{\bar x}=0\qquad (i<n-1).
\]

\subsection*{5. Action of monodromy on $\pi_1$}

In this section, corresponding to Grothendieck's exposé of 19 November 1968, we use the semistable reduction theorem for curves. Artin and Winters gave a direct proof; it can also be deduced from the analogous theorem for abelian varieties. We also use Schlessinger's theory as presented in Rim's Exposé VI.

Let $k$ be algebraically closed, let $C$ be a projective curve over $k$, and let $x_0,\ldots,x_n$ be finitely many closed points of $C$. The pointed curve
\[
X=(C;x_0,\ldots,x_n)
\]
is called \emph{stable} if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $C$ is reduced and its only singularities are ordinary double points with distinct tangents;
\item the points $x_i$ are distinct smooth points of $C$;
\item if $C_j$ is an irreducible component of $C$, and $E$ is the set of points of the normalization $\widetilde C_j$ lying over a singular point of $C$ or over one of the $x_i$, then if $C_j$ has genus $0$, respectively $1$, the set $E$ has at least $3$, respectively at least $1$, elements.
\end{enumerate}
Condition (c) is equivalent to the absence of non-trivial infinitesimal automorphisms. It is also equivalent to ampleness of the dualizing sheaf twisted by the marked points:
\[
\omega\Bigl(\sum x_i\Bigr).
\]

A curve $C$ over a scheme $S$, equipped with sections $x_0,\ldots,x_n:S\to C$, is a stable curve over $S$ if its geometric fibres are stable. The extension from the unmarked case to the marked case is formal:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item for $X/k$ stable, $\omega^n$ is very ample for $n\geq3$;
\item the formal moduli scheme of $X$ is smooth, and the singularity locus of $C$ lies over a normal-crossings divisor in the moduli space;
\item for stable curves $X$ and $Y$ over $S$, the scheme $\Isom(X,Y)$ is finite and unramified over $S$, with isomorphisms preserving the marked points;
\item for $X/k$ stable, $\Aut_S(X)\to\Aut_S(\Pic^0(X))$ is injective.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Proposition 5.1.1.}
Let
\[
X=(C;x_0,\ldots,x_n)
\]
be stable over the generic point $\eta$ of a trait $S$, and assume $C_\eta$ smooth. There exists a finite separable extension $\eta'$ of $\eta$ and a stable curve $X'$ over the normalization $S'$ of $S$ in $\eta'$ such that
\[
X'_{\eta'}=X_\eta\otimes_\eta\eta'.
\]

Let $X$ be a geometrically connected scheme of finite type over the fraction field of a strictly local trait $S$. For a geometric point $\xi$ of $X_{\bar\eta}$ there is an exact sequence
\[
0\longto \pi_1(X_{\bar\eta},\xi)\longto
\pi_1(X,\xi)\longto \Gal(\bar\eta/\eta)\longto0,
\tag{5.2.1}
\]
and hence a homomorphism from $\Gal(\bar\eta/\eta)$ to the group of outer automorphisms of $\pi_1(X_{\bar\eta})$. Passing to the maximal prime-to-$p$ quotient gives
\[
\Gal(\bar\eta/\eta)\longto
\Autext\bigl(\pi_1^{(p')}(X_{\bar\eta})\bigr).
\tag{5.2.2}
\]
If $X_\eta$ has a rational point $x$, with geometric point $\bar x$ over it, then the exact sequence splits canonically and gives
\[
[x]:\Gal(\bar\eta/\eta)\longto
\Aut\bigl(\pi_1^{(p')}(X_{\bar\eta},\bar x)\bigr).
\tag{5.2.3}
\]

\paragraph{Theorem 5.3.}
The image of wild inertia $P$ under (5.2.2), or equivalently under (5.2.3), is finite.

For the proof, one first reduces to the case where $X$ is geometrically normal and integral. Let $\widetilde X_\eta$ be the normalization of $X_\eta$; after a finite extension of $k(\eta)$, the connected components of $\widetilde X_\eta\times_\eta\bar\eta$ may be assumed geometrically normal and integral with rational base points, and the connected components of $X_{\bar\eta}$ geometrically connected with rational base points. If $X$ is normal and $U\subset X$ is open, then $\pi_1(U)$ maps onto $\pi_1(X_{\bar\eta})$. Thus one may suppose $X_\eta$ smooth and affine. Cutting by generic hyperplane sections and using Bertini, one reduces to $X$ smooth, geometrically connected, and of dimension one.

\paragraph{Theorem 5.4.}
Let $S$ be strictly local. Let $f:X\to S$ be proper and flat, with special fibre a curve whose only non-smooth points are ordinary double points, and let $Y$ be a finite union of disjoint sections contained in the smooth locus. Let $x$ be a rational point of
\[
X_\eta=X-Y.
\]
Let $U\subset S$ be the open over which $X$ is smooth, and let $\xi$ be a geometric point of $U$. Then the image of
\[
\pi_1(U,\xi)
\]
in
\[
\Aut\bigl(\pi_1^{(p')}(X_{\bar\eta},\bar x)\bigr)
\]
is abelian and prime to $p$.

One must note that $\pi_1(U,\xi)$ can act on the prime-to-$p$ fundamental group only because the prime-to-$p$ fundamental groups satisfy the specialization theorem.

\subsection*{6. Appendix: arithmetic proof of the stable reduction theorem}

Let $S$ be a trait and let $A_\eta$ be an abelian variety over $k(\eta)$ of dimension $d\neq0$. For a local morphism of traits $S'\to S$, denote by $A_{\eta'}$ the abelian variety obtained by extension of scalars, by $A_{S'}$ its Néron model over $S'$, by $A_{s'}$ its special fibre, and by $A^0_{s'}$ the neutral component of $A_{s'}$.

\paragraph{Theorem 6.1.}
For a suitable $S'$, the variety $A_{\eta'}$ has stable reduction: the group $A^0_{s'}$ is an extension of an abelian variety by a torus.

It follows fairly easily that one may take $S'$ so that $k(\eta')$ is a finite separable extension of $k(\eta)$. Suppose first that the residue field of $S$ is of finite type over the prime field. Let $\ell$ be a prime different from the residual characteristic exponent $p$, let
\[
T_\ell(A)
\]
be the Tate module, let
\[
V_\ell(A)=T_\ell(A)\otimes\QQ_\ell,
\]
and let
\[
\rho:\Gal(\bar\eta/\eta)\to\GL(V_\ell(A))
\]
be the natural representation. After a preliminary scalar extension, assume that inertia acts unipotently, hence through its largest pro-$\ell$ quotient $\ZZ_\ell(1)$. Let $T$ be the image in $\GL(V_\ell(A))$ of a generator of $\ZZ_\ell(1)$, and let $r$ be the largest integer $\geq0$ such that
\[
(T-1)^r\neq0.
\]

\paragraph{Lemma 6.2.}
The map
\[
(c,v)\in I\times V_\ell(A)\longmapsto (\rho(c)-1)^r v
\]
induces morphisms and an isomorphism
\[
V_\ell(A)\otimes\QQ_\ell(r)
\longto
V_\ell(A)/\Ker(T-1)^r\otimes\QQ_\ell(r)
\overset{\sim}{\longto}
V_\ell(A).
\tag{6.2.1}
\]

An $\ell$-adic representation $\rho:\Gal(\bar s/s)\to\GL(V)$ is said to have \emph{weight} $n$ if, for a suitable model $X$ of $k(s)$ and every closed point $x$ of $X$, the eigenvalues of geometric Frobenius $F_x$ are algebraic numbers all of whose complex conjugates have absolute value
\[
|k(x)|^{n/2}.
\]
If $V$ has weight $n$ and $W$ has weight $m\neq n$, then
\[
\Hom_{\Gal(\bar s/s)}(V,W)=0.
\]

By the universal property of the Néron model, $V_\ell(A_s^0)$ is an extension of a representation of dimension $2a$ and weight $-1$ by a representation of dimension $m$ and weight $-2$, where $a$ is the dimension of the largest abelian quotient of $A_s^0$ and $m$ the dimension of its multiplicative part. For the dual abelian variety $A^*$, the duality
\[
V_\ell(A^*)\times V_\ell(A)\to \QQ_\ell(1)
\]
shows that $V_\ell(A)$ is an extension of a representation of dimension $m$ and weight $0$ by one of dimension $2a^*$ and weight $-1$.

Since $\QQ_\ell(r)$ has weight $-2r$ and $\operatorname{Im}(T-1)^r\neq0$, the preceding weight argument and (6.2.1) imply $r\leq1$. If $r=0$, then $m=0$, $a=d$, and $A$ has good reduction. If $r=1$, then $\operatorname{Im}(T-1)$ has weight $-2$ and the corresponding quotient has weight $0$; the two spaces have the same dimension $m\leq d$. One obtains
\[
\dim V_\ell(A^*)=2a^*+m^*=2a+m,
\]
and
\[
2\dim A^0_{s'}\geq 2(a+m)>2a+m+m=2d=2\dim A_{\eta'}.
\]
Thus $A^0_{s'}$ has stable reduction, and at the same time one gets $m^*=m$.

For the general case, one reduces to a situation in which the appropriate hypotheses of the preceding equal- or unequal-characteristic propositions hold, the group $\Gal(\bar\eta/\eta)$ acts trivially on $A_\ell$, and inertia acts unipotently on $T_\ell(A)$. The same argument then applies.

\subsection*{Bibliography}
\begin{enumerate}[label={[\arabic*]}]
\item M. Artin, \emph{Algebraic approximation of structures over complete local rings}, Publ. Math. IHÉS \textbf{36} (1969), 23--58.
\item M. Artin and G. Winters, \emph{Degenerate fibres and reduction of curves}, Ann. of Math. \textbf{88} (1968), 492--517.
\item P. Deligne and D. Mumford, \emph{The irreducibility of the space of curves of a given genus}, Publ. Math. IHÉS \textbf{36} (1969), 75--110.
\item J.-P. Serre, \emph{Corps locaux}, Publ. Math. Univ. Nancago -- Hermann, 1962.
\item J.-P. Serre and J. Tate, \emph{Good reduction of abelian varieties}, Ann. of Math. \textbf{88} (1968), 492--517.
\end{enumerate}


\clearpage
\section*{SGA 7-I, Exposé II through Exposé VII.2.1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{SGA 7-I, Exposé II through Exposé VII.2.1}

\section*{SGA 7-I, Expose II -- Finiteness Properties of the Fundamental Group}

\begin{center}
\textit{Michele Raynaud}
\end{center}

Let $k$ be a separably closed field of characteristic exponent $p\ge 0$, let $X$ be a connected scheme of finite type over $k$, and let
\[
 \pi_1^{(p')}(X)
\]
be the largest quotient of $\pi_1(X)$ which is prime to $p$. We prove in this expose that, if $X$ is a surface, $\pi_1^{(p')}(X)$ is topologically finitely presented, and that the same conclusion holds for arbitrary $X$ if one assumes resolution of singularities in the relevant dimensions.

In the case of a surface, the proof, due to J.-P. Murre, reduces to the case where one has a fibration over $\mathbb P^1$ satisfying the three conditions in the following proposition.

\begin{proposition}
Let $k$ be algebraically closed, and let $S$ be a projective, irreducible, smooth surface over $k$. Then one can find a blow-up
\[
 g:S'\to S,
\]
where $S'$ is a regular surface endowed with a fibration
\[
 f:S'\to \mathbb P^1,
\]
such that $f$ has a section $\sigma$ and the following conditions hold:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $f$ is smooth at the points of $\sigma(\mathbb P^1)$;
\item the fibres of $f$ are geometrically integral curves having only ordinary singularities;
\item there exists a non-empty open subset $U$ of $\mathbb P^1$ such that the fibres of $f$ at the points of $U$ are smooth.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

The proposition follows from the existence of a projective embedding $S\hookrightarrow \mathbb P^r$ for which one can find a Lefschetz pencil. Let
\[
D=\{H_t\}_{t\in \mathbb P^1}
\]
be such a pencil of hyperplanes. Recall that it has the following properties: its axis $A$ meets $S$ transversally, so that the closed subscheme $A\cap S$ is reduced and consists of finitely many closed points $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ of $S$; there is a non-empty open set $U\subset \mathbb P^1$ such that, for $t\in U$, $H_t$ meets $S$ transversally; and for $t\in \mathbb P^1-U$, $H_t$ meets $S$ transversally except at one point, which is an ordinary quadratic singular point.

Through each point $x$ of $S$ not lying on $A$ there passes a unique hyperplane $H_t$. The map which sends $x$ to this $t\in\mathbb P^1$ is a rational map from $S$ to $\mathbb P^1$ with domain of definition $S-(A\cap S)$.

Let $g:S'\to S$ be the blow-up of the closed subscheme $S\cap A$. The scheme $S'$ is regular and the fibre $S_i$ over a point $x_i$ identifies with the projective bundle defined by the tangent space to $S$ at $x_i$, hence is isomorphic to $\mathbb P^1$. Moreover the rational map $f=a\circ g$ is everywhere defined. Finally, to each point $x_i$ one associates the section $\sigma_i$ of $f$ which sends a point $t\in\mathbb P^1$ to the tangent vector at $x_i$ to $H_t\cap S$.

We verify conditions \textup{(i)}, \textup{(ii)}, and \textup{(iii)}. For each $t\in\mathbb P^1$, the curves $S_t$ and $S'_t=g^{-1}(S_t)$ are isomorphic: the morphism $S'_t\to S_t$ is an isomorphism except possibly at the points $x_i$, and these are regular points of $S_t$; since $g$ is proper, it follows that $S'_t$ is geometrically integral. This proves \textup{(ii)}. Assertion \textup{(i)} follows from the fact that $f$ is smooth along $\sigma(\mathbb P^1)$, since $g$ is an isomorphism in a neighbourhood of $\sigma(\mathbb P^1)$ and $a$ is smooth at the points where the corresponding hyperplane meets $S$ transversally.

\begin{corollary}
With the notation of the proposition, let $Y$ be a normal-crossings divisor in $S$, and let $Y'=Y\times_S S'$. Then $f$, $g$, and $U$ may be chosen so that $Y'$ is a normal-crossings divisor relative to $U$.
\end{corollary}

Let $G$ be a profinite group. Recall that $G$ is said to be \emph{topologically finitely generated} if there is an integer $n$ such that $G$ is isomorphic to a quotient of the free profinite group $F(n)$ on $n$ generators. Let $K=\Ker(F(n)\to G)$. We say that $G$ is \emph{topologically finitely presented} if, in addition, there are finitely many elements $k_1,\ldots,k_m$ of $K$ such that the smallest closed normal subgroup of $F(n)$ containing $k_1,\ldots,k_m$ is equal to $K$.

\begin{theorem}
Let $k$ be a separably closed field of characteristic exponent $p\ge0$, and let $X$ be a connected scheme of finite type over $k$. Suppose that schemes of finite type and of dimension at most $\dim(X)$ over an algebraic closure of $k$ are strongly desingularizable. This hypothesis holds for $\dim X=2$ by Abhyankar, and for $k=\mathbb C$ by Hironaka. Then the fundamental group
\[
 \pi_1^{(p')}(X)
\]
is topologically finitely presented.
\end{theorem}

By SGA 1, IX, 4.10, one may suppose that $k$ is algebraically closed.

\paragraph{Reduction to the case where $X$ is a regular surface.}
If $f:X'\to X$ is a morphism of finite type, put $X''=X'\times_X X'$, and denote by $\pi_0(X')$ and $\pi_0(X'')$ the sets of connected components of $X'$ and $X''$, respectively. When $f$ is a morphism of effective descent for the category of etale coverings, the fundamental group $\pi_1(X)$ can be computed explicitly in terms of the fundamental groups of the connected components of $X'$ and $X''$. It follows from SGA 1, IX, 5.2 and 5.3 that, if the $\pi_1(X'_\alpha)$ are topologically finitely generated, then so is $\pi_1(X)$; and if the $\pi_1(X'_\alpha)$ are topologically finitely presented while the $\pi_1(X''_\beta)$ are topologically finitely generated, then $\pi_1(X)$ is topologically finitely presented.

Since $X$ is desingularizable, one can find a regular scheme $X'$ and a proper birational morphism $f:X'\to X$. This morphism is of effective descent for etale coverings, so it is enough to prove the theorem under the additional hypothesis that $X$ is regular.

Let $(U_i)_{i\in I}$ be a finite cover of $X$ by affine open subsets, and let $f:\coprod U_i\to X$ be the canonical morphism. Since this is again a morphism of effective descent for etale coverings, it is enough to prove the theorem for the $U_i$. Thus one is reduced to the case where $X$ is affine and smooth.

Assume from now on that $X$ is affine and smooth over $k$, with $\dim X>2$. One can apply the Lefschetz theorem for quasi-projective schemes: if $Y$ is a sufficiently general hyperplane section of $X$, then
\[
 \pi_1(Y)\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_1(X)
\]
is an isomorphism. Hence it is enough to prove the theorem for $Y$, and this reduces the argument to the case $\dim X=2$.

\paragraph{The case where $X$ is a smooth surface over $k$.}
By the strong desingularization theorem for surfaces, one can find a smooth integral projective surface $S$ and an open immersion $i:X\hookrightarrow S$ such that the divisor $Y=S-X$ has normal crossings. Apply the preceding corollary and keep its notation.

It is enough to prove that the fundamental group of $X'=X\times_S S'$ is topologically finitely presented. Since $X$ and $X'$ are regular and
\[
\operatorname{codim}_{X'}\left(X'-\bigcup_{1\le i\le n}x_i\right)\ge2,
\]
every etale covering of $X'$ induces on $X'-\bigcup_{1\le i\le n}x_i$ an etale covering which extends uniquely to an etale covering of $X$ by SGA 2, 1.11. This gives an isomorphism
\[
 \pi_1(X')\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_1(X).
\]

Consider the cartesian diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
X'&\longleftarrow&X\\
\downarrow\rlap{$h$}&&\downarrow\\
\mathbb P^1&\xleftarrow{\;j\;}&U.
\end{array}
\]
Here $h=f|_{X'}$. Let $L$ be the set of primes distinct from $p$, and let $\bar\eta$ be a geometric point above the generic point $\eta$ of $\mathbb P^1$. We verify the hypotheses of SGA 1, XIII, 4.8. The morphism $h$ has a section because $f$ does. Condition a) of SGA 1, XIII, 4.7 follows as follows. Since the fibres of $h$ are geometrically integral, $h$ is $0$-acyclic and locally $0$-acyclic. It remains to show that, for every locally constant sheaf of sets $F$ on $X'$, the pair $(F,h)$ is cohomologically proper in dimension $\le0$. If $\xi$ is a closed point of $\mathbb P^1$ and if $Z$ denotes the integral closure of $\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1,\xi}$ in $\bar\eta$, then $\mathcal X=X'\times_{\mathbb P^1}Z$ satisfies condition $(S_2)$ at the closed points of $X'$ and is regular at every other point, hence is normal. It follows that an etale covering of $\mathcal X_\xi$ whose restriction to $X'_\xi$ is connected is itself necessarily connected.

Since $X'$ is the complement in $S'$ of a normal-crossings divisor relative to $U$, the morphism $h_\eta$ is locally $1$-aspherical for $L$, and, for every finite constant sheaf of $L$-groups $F$ on $X'$, the sheaf $\underline{R}^1h_*(F_\eta)$ is cohomologically proper in dimension $\le1$. This gives condition b). Because $\mathbb P^1$ is normal and $T=\mathbb P^1-U$ has codimension $1$, condition c) follows from the etale depth inequality at $\bar s$; the topological depth condition at the corresponding points of $X'$ follows from purity; and since $h$ is smooth at $\sigma(t)$ for every $t\in\mathbb P^1$, the local $1$-asphericity condition along the section gives condition d).

By SGA 1, XIII, 4.7, if $a$ is a geometric point of $X'$, one has an exact sequence
\[
1\longrightarrow \pi_1(X'_{\bar\eta},a)\longrightarrow \pi_1(X',a)\longrightarrow \pi_1(U,\bar\eta)\longrightarrow1,
\]
and the choice of the section $\sigma$ defines a splitting of this exact sequence. Let $K$ be the image of $\pi_1(U,a)$ in $\Aut(\pi_1^{(p')}(X'_{\bar\eta},a))$. The coinvariant group $\pi_{1,\mathrm{ad}}^{(p')}(X'_{\bar\eta},a)$ is the quotient of $\pi_1^{(p')}(X'_{\bar\eta},a)$ by the closed normal subgroup generated by elements of the form $\alpha(x)x^{-1}$, with $x\in\pi_1^{(p')}(X'_{\bar\eta},a)$ and $\alpha\in K$. By loc. cit. 4.7.4, one has an isomorphism
\[
 \pi_1^{(p')}(X',a)\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{1,\mathrm{ad}}^{(p')}(X'_{\bar\eta},a).
\]

Let $\mathbb P^1-U=\{t_1,\ldots,t_n\}$. For each $i$, let $T_i$ be the strict localization of $\mathbb P^1$ at a geometric point $\bar t_i$ above $t_i$, and let $\eta_i$ be the generic point of $T_i$. An etale covering of $U$ whose restriction to every $\eta_i$ is trivial extends to an etale covering of $\mathbb P^1$, hence is trivial. Equivalently, $\pi_1(U,a)$ is the closed normal subgroup generated by the images of the groups $\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{k(t_i)}/k(t_i))$ in $\pi_1(U,a)$. By Expose I, 5.3, the image $K_i$ of $\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{k(t_i)}/k(t_i))$ in $\Aut(\pi_1^{(p')}(X'_{\bar\eta},a))$ is finite. Since $K$ is the smallest closed normal subgroup containing all $K_i$, and since $\pi_1^{(p')}(X'_{\bar\eta})$ is topologically finitely presented by SGA 1, XIII, 3.2, this proves that $\pi_1^{(p')}(X',a)$, and hence $\pi_1^{(p')}(X)$, is topologically finitely presented.

\begin{remark}
Resolution of singularities is not needed for the theorem in the case where $X$ is the open complement of a normal-crossings divisor in a smooth projective scheme over $k$.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Bibliography to Expose II}
\begin{enumerate}[label={\textup{[\arabic*]}}]
\item S. Abhyankar, \emph{Local uniformization of algebraic surfaces over ground field of characteristic $p=0$}, Amer. J. Math. 63 (1956).
\item H. Hironaka, \emph{Resolution of singularities of an algebraic variety over a field of characteristic zero}, Ann. of Math. 79 (1964).
\item M. Raynaud, \emph{Theoreme de Lefschetz en cohomologie des faisceaux coherents et en cohomologie etale}, thesis, to appear.
\end{enumerate}

\clearpage
\section*{SGA 7-I, Expose VI -- Formal Deformation Theory}

\begin{flushright}
\textit{by D. S. Rim}
\end{flushright}

\subsection*{1. Formal existence theorem}

We recall the notion of cofibered category (S.G.A. 1 IV). Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a fixed category. A \emph{cofibered category} $A$ over $\mathcal{C}$ is, by definition, a category $A$ together with a functor $P: A \to \mathcal{C}$ such that :

\textbf{Ax.~1.} For any map $f$ in $\mathcal{C}$ and any object $a$ in $A$ with $P(a) =$ the source of $f$, there exists an $f$-morphism $\alpha: a \to b$ which is cocartesian (i.e. for any $f$-morphism $\alpha': a \to b'$ there exists a unique $1$-morphism $\tau: b \to b'$ in $A$ such that $\alpha' = \tau \alpha$ where $1 =$ the target of $f$).

\textbf{Ax.~2.} A composite of cocartesian morphisms in $A$ is again cocartesian.

The following properties of a cofibered category $P: A \to \mathcal{C}$ follow immediately from the definitions :

\textbf{(cancellation)} Let $\alpha, \beta: a \rightrightarrows a'$ be any two cocartesian maps such that $P(\alpha) = P(\beta)$. If there exists a cocartesian map $\gamma: b \to a$ such that $\alpha\gamma = \beta\gamma$, then $\alpha = \beta$.

\textbf{(factorization)} Given cocartesian maps $\alpha: a \to a'$, $\beta: a \to a''$, there exists a cocartesian map $\delta: a' \to a'''$ such that $\delta\alpha = \gamma\beta$.

\subsubsection{}
\textbf{Ex. 1.1. (a)} Let $F: \mathcal{C} \to$ (categories) be a functor. Define the category $\int F$ as follows : an object in $\int F$ is a pair $(S, a)$ where $S \in \mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})$ and $a \in \mathrm{ob}\,F(S)$, and a map $(S, a) \to (S', a')$ is a pair $(f, u')$ where $f: S \to S'$ is a map in $\mathcal{C}$ and $u': F(f)(a) \to a'$ is a map in $F(S')$. Composition of two maps $(S, a) \xrightarrow{(f, u')} (S', a') \xrightarrow{(g, u'')} (S'', a'')$ is given by $(gf, u'' \cdot F(g)(u')): (S, a) \to (S'', a'')$. With respect to the functor $P: \int F \to \mathcal{C}$ given by $(S, a) \mapsto S$, $\int F$ becomes a cofibered category over $\mathcal{C}$. If $F(S)$ is a groupoid for every $S \in \mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})$, then $\int F$ is a cofibered groupoid over $\mathcal{C}$.

In particular, a functor $F: \mathcal{C} \to$ (groups) yields a cofibered group, and a functor $F: \mathcal{C} \to$ (Ens) yields a cofibered discrete groupoid. We also note that a natural transformation $\theta: F \to G$, where $F, G: \mathcal{C} \to$ (categories) are functors, determines a cocartesian functor $\int F \to \int G$.

\textbf{Ex. 1.1. (b)} Let $X_k$, $Y_k$ be schemes over $k$ and let there be given an isomorphism $X \otimes k \xrightarrow{\sim} Y \otimes k$. Then the functor $\Isom_{X,Y}: \mathcal{C}_\Lambda \to$ (groupoids) given by
\[
\Isom_{X,Y}(S) = \text{the set of isomorphisms } X \otimes S \xrightarrow{\sim} Y \otimes S \text{ such that } \cdots
\]
yields a cofibered groupoid $P: \int\Isom_{X,Y} \to \mathcal{C}_\Lambda$.

\textbf{Ex. 1.1. (c)} Let $X_0$ be a fixed scheme over $k$, and let $\mathcal{M}_X$ be the category consisting of deformations of $X_0$ over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ (see \S 4). Thus an object in $\mathcal{M}_X$ is a pair $(R, X)$ where $R$ is in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, and $X$ is a deformation of $X_0$ to $R$. Then the functor $P: \mathcal{M}_X \to \mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ given by $(R, X) \mapsto R$ defines a cofibered groupoid over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$.

\textbf{Ex. 1.1. (d)} For any groupoid $\mathcal{X}$ we denote by $[\mathcal{X}]$ the discrete groupoid consisting of the isomorphism classes of objects in $\mathcal{X}$. If $P: F \to \mathcal{C}$ is a cofibered groupoid, we obtain a functor $[F]: \mathcal{C} \to$ (Ens) given by $[F](S) = [F(S)]$, and in turn a cofibered discrete groupoid, which is, by abuse of notation, denoted by $[F]$.

\begin{definition}
A cofibered groupoid $A$ over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ is called \emph{semi-homogeneous} if the following properties hold :
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item every diagram of solid arrows in $A$ where $a' \to a$ is surjective can be completed into a commutative diagram including dotted arrows.
\item Let $R \times_k k[\epsilon]$ be the projection maps, where $k[\epsilon]$ is the ring of dual numbers over $k$, and let $a' \rightrightarrows a$ be $\pi_1$-$\pi_2$-map in $A$. Then there exists a $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$-map $\gamma: a' \to a''$ with $\alpha = \beta\gamma$ if and only if $(\pi_1)_*(a') = (\pi_2)_*(a'')$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
A reformulation of the notion in terms of $2$-categories will be given later in 2.6(b). We note that if $A$ is semi-homogeneous, then so is $[A]$ and in particular 1.2(2) entails the canonical map
\[
[A(R \times_k k[\epsilon])] \longrightarrow [A(R)] \times [A(k[\epsilon])]
\]
to be bijective.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
Consider a commutative diagram in a cofibered category $A$ over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$. Set $R = P(a)$, $R' = P(a')$ and $R_i = P(a_i)$ for $i = 1, 2$. If we set $a'''$ to be the ``direct image'' under the map $(P(\nu_1), P(\nu_2)): R' \to R_1 \times R_2$, we obtain a commutative diagram. Thus 1.2(1) can be replaced by :

(1)' every diagram in $A$ above $R_1 \leftarrow R' \to R_2$ in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, where $R' \to R_1 \times R_2$ is surjective can be completed to a commutative diagram, where $\mu_i$ ($i = 1, 2$) are projections.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
If $A$ is a semi-homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, then $[A(k[\epsilon])]$ is canonically provided with a vector space structure over $k$, where $k[\epsilon]$ is the ring of dual numbers over $k$. Indeed, let $\mathcal{V}$ be the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces over $k$. Then any functor $H: \mathcal{V} \to$ (Ens) preserving the product is canonically factored through $\mathcal{V} \to$ (Ens), where $\mathcal{V} \to$ (Ens) is the forgetful functor. On the other hand, the functor $G: \mathcal{V} \to \mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ given by $W \mapsto k[W] = k \oplus W$, with $w^2 = 0$, transforms products in $\mathcal{V}$ into fibre products over $k$ in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$. If $A$ is a semi-homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, then $[A(k[V] \times_k k[W])] \to [A(k[V])] \times [A(k[W])]$ is bijective by 1.3(a), and the functor $V \mapsto [A(k[V])]$ commutes with products, hence the result.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item Let $R \in \mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C}_\Lambda)$. A \emph{small extension} of $R$ in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ is a surjective map $R' \to R$ in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ such that $(\Ker f)^2 = 0$ and $\Ker f \cong k$.

In other words, $R' \to R$ in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ is a small extension of $R$ if
\[
0 \longrightarrow k \xrightarrow{\;t\;} R' \xrightarrow{\;f\;} R \longrightarrow 0
\]
is exact for some $t \in R'$ with $t^2 = 0$.

\item Let $A$ be a cofibered groupoid over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, and let $a$ be an object in $A$. A \emph{small prolongation} of $a$ is an arrow $a' \to a$ in $A$ such that $P(a' \to a)$ is a small extension in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$. An arrow $a' \to a$ in $A$ will be called \emph{versal for small prolongations of $a$} if, for every small prolongation $a_1 \to a$ in $A$, there exists a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
a' & \longrightarrow & a_1 \\
\searrow & & \swarrow \\
& a &
\end{array}
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
Let $A$ be a semi-homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$.

\textnormal{(1)} Let $R \times_k k[\epsilon] \xrightarrow{\pi_1} R$ be projection maps in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, and let $a \xrightarrow{\phi} a'$ be a $1$-map. Then there exists a map $a \xrightarrow{\psi} a'$ such that $\phi \otimes \beta = 1_a$ if and only if there exists a map $a' \xrightarrow{\theta} (\pi_2)_*(a'')$ such that $\cdots$

\textnormal{(2)} Let $R' \to R$ be a small extension in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, and consider a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
R' \times_R R' & & \\
\downarrow & \searrow & \\
a'' & \longrightarrow & a \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
R' & \longrightarrow & R
\end{array}
\]
Define the canonical map $\mu: R' \times_R R' \to k[\Ker f]$ by $(r_1, r_2) \mapsto r_2 - r_1$. Then, if there exists a map $a' \to (f)_*(a'')$, then there exists a map $\gamma: a' \to a$ such that $\alpha = a\gamma$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) The part ``only if'' is clear. Now assume that there exists a map $\phi: a \to (\pi_2)_* a'$. If we set $h: R \to R \times_k k[\epsilon]$ by $h = 1 \times P(\phi)$, then the composite map $R \xrightarrow{h} R \times_k k[\epsilon] \xrightarrow{\pi_1} R$ is the identity, and therefore we get maps $a \xrightarrow{\gamma} a'$ such that $\pi_1\gamma = 1_a$ where $P(\gamma) = h$ and $P(a') = \pi_1$.

On the other hand, $P(\alpha) = P(\pi_2\phi)$ and hence there exists a $1$-map $\psi: a' \to (\pi_2)_* a''$. This means that $(\pi_2)_*\psi\alpha = (\pi_2)_*\phi$, and therefore by 1.2(2) we have a $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$-map $u: h_* a \to a'$ such that $\alpha u = a_1$. Define $\beta: a \to a'$ by $\beta = u\gamma$. Then $\phi\beta = \alpha u \gamma = \pi_1\gamma = 1_a$.

(2) The map $1 \times f: R' \times_R R' \to R' \times_k k[\Ker f]$ is an isomorphism compatible with the projections on the first factor, and $\pi_2 \circ (1 \times f) = \mu$ where $\pi_2: R' \times_k k[\Ker f] \to k[\Ker f]$ is the projection map. We note that $k[\Ker f] \cong k[\epsilon]$ since $R' \to R$ is a small extension. Therefore, if there exists a map $a'' \to (\mu)_*(a')$, then by (1) above there exists a map $\gamma': a' \to a''$ such that $a' \cdot \gamma' = 1_{a'}$. Define $\gamma: a' \to a$ by $\gamma = \beta\gamma'$. We then have $a\gamma = a$.
\qed\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Let $A$ be a semi-homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ and consider a diagram in $A$ where $a_1$ is a small prolongation. Assume that $a$ is versal for small prolongations of $e$. Then there exists a map $a' \to a_1$ such that $a = a_1\theta$ in $A$ if and only if there exists a map $f: P(a') \to P(a_1)$ such that $P(a) = P(a_1) \circ f$ in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Assume that $P(a) = P(a_1) \circ f$ where $f: P(a') \to P(a_1)$. Replacing $a'$ by its direct image under $f$, we may and shall assume that $P(a') = P(a_1)$. Set $R' = P(a_1)$, $R = P(a)$. Since $A$ is semi-homogeneous, we get a commutative diagram. Since $a$ is versal for small prolongations of $e$, so is $a'$ and therefore our statement follows from 1.5(2).
\qed\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
For each object $R$ in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ we put $\Omega_R = \mathfrak{m}_\Lambda/(\mathfrak{m}_\Lambda R + \mathfrak{M})$ where $\mathfrak{m}_\Lambda$, $\mathfrak{M}$ are the maximal ideals of $\Lambda$, $R$ respectively. It is a finite-dimensional vector space over $k$, and $R \mapsto \Omega_R$ defines a functor $\Omega: \mathcal{C}_\Lambda \to \mathcal{V}$ where $\mathcal{V}$ is the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces over $k$. If $A$ is a cofibered category over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, then the composite functor $A \xrightarrow{P} \mathcal{C}_\Lambda \xrightarrow{\Omega} \mathcal{V}$ is, by abuse of notation, denoted by $\Omega_A$ again.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
Let $A$ be a semi-homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$. Then, given an object $a$ in $A$ versal for small prolongations of $e$, there exists $a' \to a$ such that :
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $a'$ is versal for small prolongations of $a$ ;
\item $P(a') \to P(a)$ is surjective ; and the kernel is annihilated by the maximal ideal of $P(a)$.
\item $\Omega_A: e \to \Omega_a$ is an isomorphism.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Set $R = P(a)$ and consider an exact sequence $0 \to J \to S \to R \to 0$ where $S$ is a formal power-series ring over $\Lambda$ in a finite number of variables, and $J \subset \mathfrak{M}$ where $\mathfrak{M}$ is the maximal ideal of $S$. Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the set of ideals $J_\alpha$ in $S$ such that (i) $J \supset J_\alpha \supset \mathfrak{M}J$ and (ii) there exists $\pi_\alpha$-morphism $a_\alpha \to a$ in $A$ where $\pi_\alpha: S/J_\alpha \to R$ is the canonical map. The property 1.2(1) of $A$ together with the fact that $J/\mathfrak{M}J$ is a finite dimensional vector space over $k$ entails that there exists a smallest ideal in the family $\mathcal{F}$. Let it be $J'$ and choose a $\pi'$-morphism $a' \to a$.
\qed\end{proof}

\subsubsection{}
We now consider the category $\mathrm{pro}(\mathcal{C}_\Lambda)$ of pro-objects in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$. If $A$ is a cofibered category over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, we obtain a cofibered category $\mathrm{pro}(A)$ over $\mathrm{pro}(\mathcal{C}_\Lambda)$ (in groupoids). For a cofibered category $A$ over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$, we define :
\[
\hat{A} = \text{the category of formal objects of } A \text{ over } \mathrm{pro}(\mathcal{C}_\Lambda).
\]

\begin{definition}
An object $\xi$ in $\hat{A}$ is called a \emph{versal formal object} of $A$ if every surjective map $q: a' \to a$ in $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ and every map $\xi \to a$ in $\hat{A}$ can be completed to a commutative diagram.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
A versal formal object $\xi$ in $\hat{A}$ is called \emph{minimally versal} if the morphism $\bar{\xi}: \Spf(\hat{R}) \to [A]$ induced by $\xi$ is a minimal embedding, where $\hat{R} = P(\xi)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Formal Existence Theorem]
A semi-homogeneous cofibered groupoid $A$ over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ which satisfies the conditions of Proposition \ref{prop:1.8} admits a minimally versal formal object, unique up to (non-unique) isomorphism.
\end{theorem}

\subsubsection{}
\textbf{Comparison with Schlessinger's conditions.} Let $F: \mathcal{C}_\Lambda \to$ (Ens) be a functor preserving the final object. The conditions (S1') and (S2) of Schlessinger are equivalent to saying that $\int F$ is semi-homogeneous. The condition (H3) of Schlessinger is equivalent to saying that $[A(k[\epsilon])]$ is a finite-dimensional vector space over $k$.

\subsection*{2. Deformation of algebraic schemes}

Let $X_0$ be a scheme over $k$, locally of finite type. We define the category $\mathcal{M}_{X_0}$ of deformations of $X_0$ over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ as follows :

\begin{definition}
An object of $\mathcal{M}_{X_0}$ over $R \in \mathcal{C}_\Lambda$ is a pair $(X, \phi)$ where $X$ is a scheme flat over $R$ and $\phi: X \otimes_R k \xrightarrow{\sim} X_0$ is an isomorphism of $k$-schemes. A morphism $(X, \phi) \to (X', \phi')$ over $R \to R'$ is a morphism $f: X \to X'$ over $R \to R'$ such that the diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
X \otimes_R k & \xrightarrow{\;\sim\;} & X_0 \\
\downarrow & & \|\\
X' \otimes_{R'} k & \xrightarrow{\;\sim\;} & X_0
\end{array}
\]
commutes.
\end{definition}

With the projection functor $P(X, \phi) = R$, $\mathcal{M}_{X_0}$ is a cofibered groupoid over $\mathcal{C}_\Lambda$.

\begin{proposition}
If $X_0$ is proper over $k$, then $\mathcal{M}_{X_0}$ is semi-homogeneous. Moreover, $[\mathcal{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])]$ is canonically isomorphic to $\Ext^1_{X_0}(\Omega^1_{X_0/k}, \cO_{X_0})$.
\end{proposition}

\subsubsection{}
For a morphism $f: X_0 \to Y_0$ of schemes over $k$, one can define the category of deformations of $f$ and prove analogous results. The obstructions to deforming $X_0$ lie in $\Ext^2_{X_0}(\Omega^1_{X_0/k}, \cO_{X_0})$.

\subsection*{3. The cotangent complex}

Let $f: X \to S$ be a morphism of schemes. The cotangent complex $\mathbb{L}_{X/S}$ is an object of the derived category $D^-(X)$ defined up to unique isomorphism.

\begin{theorem}
The cotangent complex satisfies the following properties :
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item For any commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
X' & \longrightarrow & X \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
S' & \longrightarrow & S
\end{array}
\]
there is a canonical base-change morphism $\mathbb{L}g^* \mathbb{L}_{X/S} \to \mathbb{L}_{X'/S'}$ which is an isomorphism when the diagram is cartesian and $X \to S$ is flat.
\item For any composition $X \xrightarrow{f} Y \xrightarrow{g} Z$, there is a distinguished triangle
\[
g^* \mathbb{L}_{Y/Z} \longrightarrow \mathbb{L}_{X/Z} \longrightarrow \mathbb{L}_{X/Y} \longrightarrow g^* \mathbb{L}_{Y/Z}[1].
\]
\item $\mathcal{H}^0(\mathbb{L}_{X/S}) \cong \Omega^1_{X/S}$.
\item If $f$ is smooth, then $\mathbb{L}_{X/S} \cong \Omega^1_{X/S}[0]$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\subsection*{4. Deformations of ordinary quadratic singularities}

Let $X_0$ be a scheme over $k$ with only ordinary quadratic singularities. An ordinary quadratic singularity is, locally for the etale topology, isomorphic to the singularity at the origin of the cone $\Spec k[x_1, \ldots, x_n]/(x_1^2 + \cdots + x_n^2)$.

\begin{proposition}
Let $X_0$ be as above. Then :
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The deformation functor of the singularity is smooth (i.e., unobstructed).
\item The miniversal deformation space is isomorphic to $\Spf(k[[t]])$, where the parameter $t$ corresponds to the deformation of the singularity.
\item In a miniversal deformation, the singular locus is a divisor with normal crossings.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Locally, the singularity is defined by $f = x_1^2 + \cdots + x_n^2 = 0$. The tangent space to the deformation functor is $\Ext^1(\Omega^1_{X_0}, \cO_{X_0})$ which is one-dimensional, generated by the class of $f$ itself. The obstruction space $\Ext^2(\Omega^1_{X_0}, \cO_{X_0})$ vanishes, proving (i) and (ii). For (iii), one notes that the total space of the miniversal deformation is smooth and the singular fiber is a divisor.
\qed\end{proof}

\subsection*{Conclusion}

\subsection*{5. Semi-homogeneous groupoids (continued)}

We assume that $\uA(K) = \{e\}$.

\begin{definition}
A cofibered groupoid $\uA$ over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda,K}$ is called \emph{semi-homogeneous} if the following properties hold:

\textup{(I)} Every diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
a'' & \dashrightarrow & a' \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
a_1 & \longrightarrow & a
\end{array}
\]
of solid arrows in $\uA$, where $a' \longrightarrow a$ is surjective, can be completed into a commutative diagram including dotted arrows.

\textup{(II)} For any $R$, the natural map
\[
[\uA(R \times_K D_{K'}(K))] \longrightarrow [\uA(R)] \times [\uA(D_{K'}(K))] \simeq [\uA(R)] \times [\uA(D_{K'}(K))]
\]
is bijective.
\end{definition}

If $\uA$ is semi-homogeneous, so is $[\uA]$.

\paragraph{1.17.} Let us first consider the restriction of $\uA$ to the full subcategory of $\underline{C}_{\Lambda,K}$ whose objects are the $D_V(K)$. Let $\Omega$ be the vector space $\Omega^1_{K/\Lambda} \otimes_K K'$, and $d : K \to \Omega$ the derivative. A map $f : D_V(K) \to D_W(K)$ can be written:
\[
f(k + v\varepsilon) = k + (u(v) + D \cdot dk)
\]
with $u \in \Hom_{K'}(V,W)$ and $D \in \Hom_{K'}(\Omega, W)$. If we identify $f$ with $(u,D)$, the composition law is
\[
(u,D) \circ (u', D') = (uu', D + uD')\;.
\]

The condition \textup{(II)} entails

\textup{(II$'$)} The functor from $K'$-vector spaces to sets
\[
V \longmapsto [\uA(D_V(K))]
\]
commutes with products.

\begin{lemma}
Assume condition \textup{(II$'$)} is satisfied by $\uA$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(1)] $[\uA(D_{K'}(K))]$ carries a unique vector space structure, such that
\[
[\uA(D_V(K))] \simeq V \otimes_{K'} [\uA(D_{K'}(K))]
\]
as a functor in $V$.

\item[(2)] There is a linear map $\gamma : \Hom(\Omega, K') \to [\uA(D_{K'}(K))]$ such that, for any $D \in \Hom(\Omega, K')$,
\[
[\uA((k,D))] : [\uA(D_{K'}(K))] \longrightarrow [\uA(D_{K'}(K))] \text{ is } x \longmapsto x + \gamma(D)\;.
\]

\item[(3)] For any $(u,D) : D_V(K) \longrightarrow D_W(K)$, the map
\begin{align*}
(u,D) : [\uA(D_V(K))] &\longrightarrow [\uA(D_W(K))] \text{ i.e.} \\
(u,D) : V \otimes [\uA(D_{K'}(K))] &\longrightarrow W \otimes [\uA(D_{K'}(K))] \text{ is} \\
x &\longmapsto u(x) + \gamma(D)\;.
\end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

Let us introduce the notations
\begin{align*}
F(V) &= [\uA(D_V(K))] \\
A &= F(K') \\
F(u,D) : F(V) \to F(W) &= \text{the map induced by } (u,D) : D_V(K) \to D_W(K) \\
F(D) : F(V) \to F(V) &: F(D) = F(1,D)\;.
\end{align*}

By (II$'$) and the fact that $K'$ is a $K'$-vector space object in the category of $K'$-vector spaces, $F(K')$ is a $K'$-vector space in $(\Ens)$, hence a $K'$-vector space, and (1) is clear. Let us now identify $F(V)$ and $V \otimes A$.

We deduce from the identity
\[
(u,D) = (1,D) \circ (u,0)
\]
that
\[
F(u,D) = F(D) \circ (u \otimes 1_A)\;.
\]

The functoriality of $F$ amounts to
\begin{align*}
F(0) &= \text{Id} \\
F(D) \circ F(D') &= F(D + D') \\
(u \otimes 1) \circ F(D) &= F(uD) \circ (u \otimes 1_A)\;.
\end{align*}

For any $D : \Omega \to V$ and any $n \in \bN$, if $i$ and $p$ are the maps
\[
i : V \hookrightarrow V \oplus K'^n \qquad p : V \oplus K'^n \longrightarrow K'^n\;,
\]
then $F(i)^{-1} \circ F(D \circ p) \circ F(i)$ does not depend on $n$ and is equal to $F(D)$. In fact, let $\chi : K'^n \to V$ be a map, $\chi_i = \chi(e_i)$ and let $a_i$ be a family of elements of $A$. We have $(1, \chi \circ p) = (1, \sum \chi_i \circ p_i)$. Hence, if
\[
F(D)\left(\sum x_i \otimes a_i\right) = \sum x_i \otimes a_i + \alpha(D)\;,
\]
then
\[
F(D)(y) = y + \alpha(D) \quad \text{with}
\]
\begin{align*}
\alpha(D + D') &= \alpha(D) + \alpha(D') \\
\alpha(uD) &= F(uD)\quad (\text{for any } u : V \to W)\;.
\end{align*}
One easily checks that any such $\alpha$ is defined, for some $\gamma \in \Hom(\Omega, A)$, by the formula
\[
\alpha(D) = \sum D(1) \cdot \gamma(v_i)
\]
as promised in (2) (3).

\begin{definition}
\textup{(1)} An object $\xi$ in $\uA$ is called \emph{versal formal object} if every surjective map $a : a' \to a$ in $\uA$ induces a surjective map
\[
\Hom_{\uA}(\xi, a') \longrightarrow \Hom_{\uA}(\xi, a)\;.
\]

\textup{(2)} A formal versal object $\xi$ in $\uA$, with image $(R, \xi)$ in $\Ob(\uA)$, is \emph{minimal} if for any two maps $\varphi, \psi : R \to D_V(K)$, if $\varphi(\xi)$ is isomorphic to $\psi(\xi)$, then there exists a derivation $D$ of $K$ into $K'$ such that $(\psi, D) \circ \xi = \xi$, i.e., $\psi = \varphi - D \circ d$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
If $\uA$ is semi-homogeneous and if
\[
\dim_K [\uA(D_K)] < \infty\;,
\]
then $\uA$ admits a minimally versal formal object, and any two of them are isomorphic.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
With the notations of 1.18, let $H^*$ be a subspace of $[\uA(D_{K'}(K))]$, supplementary to the image of $\gamma$, and let $H$ be the dual of $H^*$. We define
\[
R_1 = D_H(K)\;,
\]
let $\xi_1$ be an object in $\uA(R_1)$, whose image in
\[
[\uA(R_1)] = H^* \otimes [\uA(D_{K'}(K))] = \Hom(H^*, [\uA(D_{K'}(K))])
\]
is the inclusion of $H^*$ in $[\uA(D_{K'}(K))]$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(1)] The maps $\Hom_{\underline{C}_{\Lambda}}(D_{K'}(K), D_V(K)) \longrightarrow [\uA(D_V(K))]$ are surjective.
\item[(2)] The minimality condition 1.19(2) is valid for $(R_1, \xi_1)$.
\end{enumerate}

Choose now some $S \in \underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, formally smooth over $\Lambda$, and an epimorphism
\[
\pi : S \to R_1
\]
such that $\Ker \pi \cap \fm_S^2 = 0$. If $\fm$ is the maximal ideal of $S$, we inductively define, for $n \geq 1$,
\[
R_n = S/\fm^n \quad \text{and} \quad \xi_n \in \Ob(\uA(R_n))
\]
by the properties that
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $\fm^n$ is the intersection of the ideals $I \supset \fm^n$ of $S$, such that $\xi_n$ extends to an object $\xi$ in $\uA(S/I)$.
\item[(b)] $\xi_n$ is an extension of $\xi_{n-1}$.
\end{enumerate}
If $R = \varprojlim R_n$ and $\xi = \varprojlim \xi_n$, then one checks that $(R, \xi)$ is a minimally versal formal object, and the only one up to isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{6. Pro-representable cofibered groupoids}

Let $\uA$ be a cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, admitting a minimally versal formal object $\xi$. In general we cannot recover $\uA$ (up to $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$-equivalence) out of $\xi$ alone.

A number of cofibered groupoids which usually occur in practice enjoy a slightly stronger property than those of semi-homogeneous ones, as it will be defined explicitly in 2.5. To investigate them it is convenient to rephrase what we have done so far in terms of ``smooth functors''. All the notations and terminologies in the previous section remain in force throughout in this section.

\begin{definition}
Let $\varphi : \uA \longrightarrow \uB$ be a functor of cofibered groupoids over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. We say that $\varphi$ is \emph{smooth} if every surjective map $\beta : b' \longrightarrow \varphi(a)$ in $\uB$ can be completed to a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
\varphi(a') & \longrightarrow & b' \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \beta \\
\varphi(a) & \longrightarrow & \varphi(a)
\end{array}
\qquad (*)
\]
for some $\alpha : a' \longrightarrow a$ in $\uA$. We say that $\varphi$ is \emph{minimally smooth} if it is smooth and $\varphi(k[\varepsilon]) : [\uA(k[\varepsilon])] \longrightarrow [\uB(k[\varepsilon])]$ is bijective.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\textup{(a)} Let $\varphi : \uA \longrightarrow \uB$ be a functor of cofibered groupoids over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. We note that ``$\varphi$ is smooth'' $\Leftrightarrow$ ``every surjective map $\beta : b' \longrightarrow \varphi(a)$ in $\uB$ can be completed into a commutative diagram $(*)$ such that $\varphi(a') \longrightarrow b'$ is a $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$-isomorphism'' $\Leftrightarrow$ ``every small prolongation $\beta : b' \longrightarrow \varphi(a)$ in $\uB$ can be completed into a commutative diagram $(*)$''.

\textup{(b)} For any cofibered groupoid $\uA$ over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, the natural functor $\uA \longrightarrow [\uA]$ is minimally smooth.

\textup{(c)} For each object $R$ in $\widehat{\underline{C}}_{\Lambda}$, we set $h_R : \underline{C}_{\Lambda} \longrightarrow (\Ens)$ to be the functor defined by $h_R(S) = \Hom_{\Lambda\text{-alg}}(R,S)$ for all $S$ in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. It associates a cofibered discrete groupoid $\underline{h}_R$ over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, and a map $R \longrightarrow S$ in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ induces a functor $\underline{h}_S \to \underline{h}_R$ of cofibered groupoids. The functor $\underline{h}_S \to \underline{h}_R$ induced by a map $R \to S$ in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ is smooth if and only if $S$ is a formal power-series ring over $R$.

\textup{(d)} Given an object $R$ in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, let us denote by $(R, \underline{C}_{\Lambda})$ the category of objects in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ over $R$. We have a canonical functor $(R, \underline{C}_{\Lambda}) \to \underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. Given $\xi \in \Ob(\uA)$ with $P(\xi) = R$, let $\uA \to (R, \underline{C}_{\Lambda})$ be the pull-back of $\uA \to \underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ under the canonical functor $(R, \underline{C}_{\Lambda}) \to \underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ and choose a section $\xi^*$ such that $\xi^*(1_R) = \xi$. (This simply means that we choose, for each map $R \to S$, an $f$-map $\xi \to \xi(f)$). We then obtain a functor $\xi^* : \underline{h}_R \to \uA$ over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ by sending $(R \to S) \mapsto \xi(f)$. Of course this functor does not depend on the choice of $\xi^*$ up to natural equivalence of functors. Now ``$\xi^* : \underline{h}_R \to \uA$ is (minimally) smooth'' means that every surjective map $a' \to \xi(f)$ in $\uA$ can be completed into a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi(f'') & \longrightarrow & a' \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
\xi(f) & \longrightarrow & \xi(f)
\end{array}
\]
(and $\underline{h}_R(k[\varepsilon]) \to \uA(k[\varepsilon])$ is bijective), which means precisely that $\xi$ is a (minimally) versal formal object of $\uA$ over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. Thus we conclude that the existence of a (minimally) versal formal object of $\uA$ is equivalent to the existence of a (minimally) smooth functor $\underline{h}_R \to \uA$ over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, for some $R$.
\end{remark}

The following general properties on smoothness are trivial to verify.

\begin{proposition}
Let $\uA \xrightarrow{\varphi} \uB \xrightarrow{\psi} \uC$ be functors of cofibered groupoids over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] If $\varphi$ and $\psi$ are smooth, so is $\psi \circ \varphi$.
\item[(b)] If $\psi$ and $\psi \circ \varphi$ are smooth, so is $\varphi$.
\item[(c)] If $\psi \circ \varphi$ is smooth and if $\varphi$ is surjective on isomorphism classes of objects, then $\psi$ is smooth.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
Let $\uA$ be a cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, admitting a versal formal object $\xi$. This means, by 2.2(d), that $\xi^* : \underline{h}_R \to \uA$ is smooth, where $R = P(\xi)$. Therefore we have, ``$\uA$ is (formally) smooth over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ (c.f.\ 1.13) i.e., $R$ is a formal power-series algebra over $\Lambda$'' $\Leftrightarrow$ ``the composite functor $\underline{h}_R \to \uA \to \underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ is smooth'' $\Leftrightarrow$ ``$\uA \to \underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ is a smooth functor'' by 2.3(b).
\end{remark}

We now consider a stronger property than being semi-homogeneous.

\begin{definition}
Let $\uA$ be a cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. It is called \emph{homogeneous} if every diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
a' & \longrightarrow & a \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
b' & \longrightarrow & b
\end{array}
\]
where $a' \to a$ is surjective admits a fibre-product $a' \times_a b'$ in $\uA$ such that
\[
P(a' \times_a b') = P(a') \times_{P(a)} P(b')\;.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\textup{(a)} Let $\uA$ be a cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, and consider a diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
a' & \longrightarrow & a \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
a_1 & \longrightarrow & a_2
\end{array}
\]
in $\uA$ above the diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
R' & \longrightarrow & R \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
R_1 & \longrightarrow & R_2
\end{array}
\]
in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. If $R' \to R$ is surjective, we obtain a unique map $\theta : a' \to a_1 \times_{a_2} a_2$ compatible with the projections. If $P(a_1 \times_{a_2} a_2) = R_1 \times_{R_2} R_2$, then $P(\theta)$ is the identity map on $R_1 \times_{R_2} R_2$ and therefore $\theta$ is $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$-isomorphism, i.e., $a' = a_1 \times_{a_2} a_2$. In other words, if $\uA$ is a homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, every diagram $(*)$ defines a fibre-product $a_1 \times_{a_2} a_2$ provided $R_1 \to R_2$ is surjective in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. This fact entails immediately that a homogeneous cofibered groupoid is, a fortiori, semi-homogeneous.

\textup{(b)} Consider a diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
& & X \\
& & \downarrow \\
Y & \longrightarrow & Z
\end{array}
\]
of categories and functors. The ordinary fibre-product $X \times_Z Y$ is a category in which an object is a pair $(x,y)$ such that $\Phi(x) = \Psi(y)$, and a map $(x,y) \to (x',y')$ consists of a pair $\alpha : x \to x'$, $\beta : y \to y'$ such that $\Phi(\alpha) = \Psi(\beta)$. The fibre-product $X \times_Z Y$ in the sense of 2-categories is a category in which an object is a triple $(x,y;u)$, where $u : \Phi(x) \to \Psi(y)$ is an isomorphism, and a map $(x,y;u) \to (x',y';u')$ consists of a pair $x \to x'$, $y \to y'$ with a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
\Phi(x) & \xrightarrow{u} & \Psi(y) \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
\Phi(x') & \xrightarrow{u'} & \Psi(y')
\end{array}
\]
One can rephrase the notions of (semi-)homogeneous cofibered groupoid or smooth functor in terms of fibre-product of categories (in the sense of 2-category). Now let $P : \uA \to \underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ be cofibered category, and consider an object $R \in \underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. If we choose a direct image functor $(\operatorname{pr}_i)_! : \uA(R_1 \times_R R_2) \to \uA(R_i)$, where $\operatorname{pr}_i : R_1 \times_R R_2 \to R_i$ is the projection for $i = 1,2$, we obtain a functor
\[
\uA(R_1 \times_R R_2) \longrightarrow \uA(R_1) \times_{\uA(R)} \uA(R_2)\;.
\]
It is clear from the definition of cofibered category that this functor, up to canonical natural equivalence, does not depend on the choice of direct image functors. It is now clear from the definition that a cofibered groupoid $\uA$ over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ is homogeneous over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ if and only if
\[
\uA(R_1 \times_R R_2) \longrightarrow \uA(R_1) \times_{\uA(R)} \uA(R_2)
\]
is an equivalence of categories whenever $R_i \to R$ is surjective in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, and is semi-homogeneous if and only if
\[
[\uA(R_1 \times_R R_2)] \longrightarrow [\uA(R_1)] \times_{[\uA(R)]} [\uA(R_2)]
\]
is bijective whenever $R_i \to R$ is surjective, and is bijective when $R_1 = R_2 = k[\varepsilon]$.

Now let $\varphi : \uA \to \uB$ be a functor of cofibered groupoids over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. For each map $R' \to R$ in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, $\varphi$ induces a functor $\uA(R') \to \uA(R) \times_{\uB(R)} \uB(R')$ uniquely determined up to a (canonical) natural equivalence. It is again trivial to see that $\varphi$ is smooth if and only if the functor $\uA(R') \to \uA(R) \times_{\uB(R)} \uB(R')$ is surjective on isomorphism classes of objects, whenever $R' \to R$ is surjective.

\textup{(c)} If $\uA$ is a homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, the functor $\uA(k[V \oplus W]) \to \uA(k[V]) \times \uA(k[W])$ is an equivalence of categories for any finite-dimensional vector spaces $V$, $W$ over $k$ (We recall our convention $\uA(k) = \{e\}$). In particular, we have
\[
\Aut_{\uA(k[V])}(1_V) \oplus \Aut_{\uA(k[W])}(1_W) \simeq \Aut_{\uA(k[V \oplus W])}(1_V \oplus 1_W)
\]
where $1_V$ is the direct image of $e$ under the unique map $k \to k[V]$ in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. It follows that if $\uA$ is homogeneous over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, $\Aut(1_V)$ as well as $[\uA(k[\varepsilon])]$ are canonically provided with vector space structures over $k$.

\textup{(d)} For any object $R$ in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, the cofibered (discrete) groupoid $\underline{h}_R$ over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ is homogeneous.

If $\uA$ is semi-homogeneous, then so is $[\uA]$. However, the homogeneity of $\uA$ does not imply in general the homogeneity of $[\uA]$. Indeed we have
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
Let $\uA$ be a homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. The following statements are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $[\uA]$ is homogeneous, i.e., $[\uA(R' \times_R S)] \to [\uA(R')] \times_{[\uA(R)]} [\uA(S)]$ is bijective for every small extension $R' \to R$.
\item[(ii)] For every small prolongation $a' \to a$ in $\uA$, the map $\Aut_{\uA(R')}(a') \to \Aut_{\uA(R)}(a)$ is surjective, where $R' = P(a')$, $R = P(a)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) $\Rightarrow$ (ii). Let $R' \xrightarrow{f} R$ be a small extension in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ and let $a' \to a$ be an $f$-map in $\uA$. Given $\tau \in \Aut_{\uA(R)}(a)$, we consider $a' \times_a a'$ and $a' \times_a a'_\tau$ where $a'_\tau \to a$ denotes the composite map $a' \to a \xrightarrow{\tau} a$. Since
\[
[\uA(R' \times_R R')] \longrightarrow [\uA(R')] \times_{[\uA(R)]} [\uA(R')]
\]
is bijective, we conclude the existence of an isomorphism $a' \times_a a' \xrightarrow{u} a' \times_a a'_\tau$, and $u$ induces, via the projection on the second factor, an isomorphism $a' \xrightarrow{\tau'} a'$ which is a lifting of $\tau$.

(ii) $\Rightarrow$ (i). Let $a'_i$ ($i = 1,2$) be objects in $\uA(R' \times_R S)$ which have the same image under $[\uA(R' \times_R S)] \to [\uA(R')] \times_{[\uA(R)]} [\uA(S)]$. We then obtain a diagram of solid arrows
\[
\begin{array}{ccccccccc}
& & & & a''_2 & & & & \\
& & & \nearrow & & \searrow & & & \\
& & a''_1 & & & & b_1 & \xrightarrow{\tau} & b_2 \\
& \swarrow & & \searrow & & \swarrow & & & \\
a'_2 & & & & a_1 & & & & \\
& \searrow & & \swarrow & & & & & \\
& & a_2 & & & & & &
\end{array}
\qquad
\begin{array}{ccc}
& R' \times_R S & \\
\swarrow & & \searrow \\
R' & & S \\
& \searrow & \swarrow \\
& R &
\end{array}
\]
where $\bar{\tau} : a_1 \to a_2$ is the induced isomorphism from $\tau$, so that the square box on the lower right corner is commutative. If the condition (ii) is verified, one can always replace the given isomorphism $a''_1 \xrightarrow{\sim} a''_2$ so that the square box on the left corner is also commutative. Since $a''_i = a'_i \times_{a_i} b_i$ for $i = 1,2$ by 2.6(a), we obtain the isomorphism $a''_1 \xrightarrow{\sim} a''_2$. This proves that $[\uA(R' \times_R S)] \longrightarrow [\uA(R')] \times_{[\uA(R)]} [\uA(S)]$ is injective, and hence is bijective since $\uA$ is homogeneous by hypothesis.
\end{proof}

Now let $\uA$ be a homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, and let $R' \xrightarrow{f} R$ be a small extension in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. We have a canonical map
\[
\Ker f \longrightarrow R' \times_R R' \quad \text{given by} \quad (r_1, r_2) \longmapsto r_1 \cdot (0) + (r_2 - r_1)\;,
\]
and $D_f : \Ker f \to R' \times_R R'[\Ker f]$ is an isomorphism. Given any two $f$-maps $a_i \to a$ ($i = 1,2$) in $\uA$ above $R_1 \times_R R_2$, we may consider $\tau(a_1, a_2) = F(a_1 \times_a a_2)$ which is an object in $\uA(k[\Ker f])$. Since $1 \times f : R_1 \times_R R_1 \to R' \times_R R'[\Ker f]$ is an isomorphism, we have the isomorphism
\[
a_1 \times_a a_2 = a_1 \times \tau(a_1, a_2)
\]
compatible with the projections on the first factor.

The statement 1.5(2) takes a more definite form for a homogeneous groupoid.

\begin{lemma}
Let $\uA$ be a homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, and $a_i \to a$ ($i = 1,2$) $f$-maps in $\uA$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(1)] There exists $p : a_1 \to a_2$ in $\uA$ with $a_2 \circ p = a_1$ if and only if there exists an arrow $a_1 \to \tau(a_1, a_2)$ in $\uA$.
\item[(2)] There exists $p : a_1 \to a_2$ with $a_2 \circ p = a_1$, such that $P(p) = \text{Id}$ if and only if $\tau(a_1, a_2) = 0$ in $[\uA(k[\Ker f])]$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We prove both at the same time. There exists $p : a_1 \to a_2$ in $\uA$ with $a_2 \circ p = a_1$ (such that $P(p) = \text{Id}$, $P(a_i) = R'$) $\Leftrightarrow$ there exists $h : a_1 \to a_1 \times \tau(a_1, a_2)$ in $\uA$ with $\operatorname{pr}_1 \circ h = \text{Id}$ (such that $P(h) = \text{diagonal map}$) $\Leftrightarrow$ there exists $h : a_1 \to a_1 \times \tau(a_1, a_2)$ in $\uA$ with $\operatorname{pr}_1 \circ h = \text{Id}$ (such that $P(h) : R' \to R' \times k[\Ker f]$ is given by $r' \mapsto (r', r'(0))$) $\Leftrightarrow$ there exists an arrow $\gamma : a_1 \to \tau(a_1, a_2)$ in $\uA$ (such that $P(\gamma) : R' \to k[\Ker f]$ is given by $r' \mapsto r'(0)$). However, there exists an arrow $\gamma : a_1 \to \tau(a_1, a_2)$ in $\uA$ such that $P(\gamma) : R' \to k[\Ker f]$ is factored through $R' \to k \to k[\Ker f]$ if and only if $\tau(a_1, a_2) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
\textup{(a)} Let $\uA \xrightarrow{\varphi} \uB \xrightarrow{\psi} \uC$ be functors of cofibered groupoids over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. Assume that
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $\uB$ is semi-homogeneous and $\psi$ is homogeneous;
\item[(ii)] $\psi \circ \varphi(k[\varepsilon]) : [\uC(k[\varepsilon])] \longrightarrow [\uC(k[\varepsilon])]$ is injective.
\end{enumerate}
Then the composite functor $\psi \circ \varphi$ is smooth if and only if $\varphi$ and $\psi$ are both smooth.

\textup{(b)} Let $\varphi : \uA \to \uB$ be a smooth functor of cofibered groupoids over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. If $\uA$ is semi-homogeneous, so is $\uB$. If $\uA \to \uB$ is smooth (i.e., for any surjective map $b' \to \varphi(a)$ in $\uB$, there exists $a : a' \to a$ in $\uA$ and a $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$-isomorphism $p : \varphi(a') \to b'$ such that $\beta \circ p = \varphi(a)$).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) Assume that the composite functor $\psi \circ \varphi$ is smooth. If $\varphi$ is smooth, then so is $\psi$ by 2.3(b), and therefore it suffices to show that $\varphi$ is smooth. Let a small prolongation $b' \xrightarrow{g} \psi(a)$ in $\uB$ be given. Since the composite functor $\psi \circ \varphi$ is smooth, there exists an arrow $a' \to a$ in $\uA$ and a $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$-isomorphism $p : \psi\varphi(a') \to b'$ with a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
\psi\varphi(a') & \longrightarrow & b' \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
\psi\varphi(a) & \longrightarrow & \psi\varphi(a)
\end{array}
\]
Since $\uB$ is semi-homogeneous, we can choose a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
\varphi(a') & \longrightarrow & b' \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
\varphi(a) & \longrightarrow & \psi(a)
\end{array}
\]
above $R' \times_R R' \to R'$, which proves that $\varphi$ is smooth.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
A cofibered groupoid $\uA$ over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ is called \emph{(minimally) pro-representable} if it is $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$-equivalent to $\underline{h}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}$ for some (normalized) cogroupoid $(R_0, R_1, \ldots)$ in $\widehat{\underline{C}}_{\Lambda}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
If $(R_0, R_1, \ldots)$ is a normalized cogroupoid in $\widehat{\underline{C}}_{\Lambda}$, then every homomorphism $(\sigma_0, \sigma_1) : (R_0, R_1, \ldots) \to (R_0, R_1, \ldots)$ which induces $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$-equivalence $\underline{h}_{(\sigma_0, \sigma_1)} : \underline{h}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)} \longrightarrow \underline{h}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}$ is an isomorphism.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
\textup{(a)} Let $\uA$ be a cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. Assume that
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $\uA$ is homogeneous;
\item[(ii)] $[\uA]$ is homogeneous;
\item[(iii)] $\dim_k [\uA(k[\varepsilon])] < \infty$.
\end{enumerate}
Then $\uA$ is minimally pro-representable.

\textup{(b)} Let $\varphi : \uA \to \uB$ be a $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$-equivalence of cofibered groupoids over $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. If $\uA$ is minimally pro-representable, so is $\uB$.

\textup{(c)} If $\uA$ is minimally pro-representable, so is $[\uA]$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(c) Since $\uA$ is minimally pro-representable, there exists a normalized cogroupoid $(R_0, R_1, \ldots)$ in $\widehat{\underline{C}}_{\Lambda}$ and a $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$-equivalence $\uA \simeq \underline{h}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}$. Then $[\uA] \simeq [\underline{h}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}] = h_{R_0}$, which is minimally pro-representable.

(a) Since $\uA$ is semi-homogeneous and $\dim [\uA(k[\varepsilon])] < \infty$, $\uA$ admits a minimally versal formal object. On the other hand, $(R_0, 1_{R_0})$ is a quasi-initial minimal object of $\widehat{\underline{h}}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}$ since $\underline{h}_{R_0}(k[\varepsilon]) \to [\widehat{\underline{h}}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}(k[\varepsilon])]$ is bijective, and therefore $(R_0, 1_{R_0})$ is a minimally versal formal object of $\widehat{\underline{h}}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}$ by 1.10(ii), which means that $e : \underline{h}_{R_0} \longrightarrow \widehat{\underline{h}}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}$ is minimally smooth.

(c) Let $(S_0, \xi_0)$ be a minimally versal formal object for $\widehat{\underline{h}}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}$ (1.11). For any object $\xi : R_0 \to T$ in $\widehat{\underline{h}}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}(T)$, the functor $\Isom(\xi, \xi)$ from $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}/T \times_T$ to (sets) which to any $R$ associates the sets of isomorphisms between the two images of $\xi$ on $R : R_0 \xrightarrow{\xi} T \xrightarrow{\longrightarrow} T \otimes_{\Lambda} T \longrightarrow R$, is represented by $R_1 \otimes_{R_0 \otimes_{\Lambda} R_0} (T \otimes_{\Lambda} T)$. By a passage to limit, one gets that $\Isom(\xi_0, \xi_0)$ is pro-represented by some $S_1$, and $\widehat{\underline{h}}_{(R_0, R_1, \ldots)}$ is equivalent to $\widehat{\underline{h}}_{(S_0, S_1, \ldots)}$. By (b), $S_*$ is minimally smooth.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{7. Cohomology}

In this section, we fix a complete noetherian local ring $\Lambda$ with residue field $k$. Let $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ be the category of artinian local $\Lambda$-algebras with residue field $k$. We also fix a functor $G$ from $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ to the category of abelian groups, which is a sheaf for the \emph{fc} (faithfully coalgebraically flat) topology. For each $R \in \underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, we denote by $\underline{C}_{R}$ the category of $R$-algebras in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$, provided with the induced topology. We denote by $\underline{C}_{R}^{\sim}$ the category of sheaves of abelian groups on $\underline{C}_{R}$.

\begin{definition}
Let $B$ be an $R$-algebra in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. An \emph{augmented simplicial object} over $B$ is a simplicial object $P_*$ in $\underline{C}_{R}$ together with an augmentation $P_* \to B$ such that
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] Each $P_n$ is a polynomial algebra over $R$ (in a finite number of variables).
\item[(ii)] The underlying sequence of sets $P_n \to B$ is exact.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
For any $B \in \underline{C}_{R}$, there exists an augmented simplicial object over $B$.
\end{proposition}

For any augmented simplicial object $P_* \to B$, we define
\[
\check{H}^n(P_*/R, F) = H^n(F(P_*))
\]
for any sheaf $F$ on $\underline{C}_{R}$. If $P_* \to B$ and $Q_* \to B$ are two augmented simplicial objects, then there exists a map $P_* \to Q_*$ over $B$, unique up to simplicial homotopy.
