Hence $\check{H}^n(P_*/R, F)$ does not depend on the choice of $P_*$, up to unique isomorphism. We shall write $\check{H}^n(B/R, F)$ instead of $\check{H}^n(P_*/R, F)$.

\begin{proposition}
For any short exact sequence $0 \to F' \to F \to F'' \to 0$ in $\underline{C}_{R}^{\sim}$, we have a long exact sequence
\[
0 \to F'(B) \to F(B) \to F''(B) \to \check{H}^1(B/R, F') \to \check{H}^1(B/R, F) \to \cdots\;.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
\textup{(a)} $\check{H}^n(P/R, F) = 0$ for all $n > 0$ and for all presheaves $F$ on $\underline{C}_{R}$, if $P$ is a polynomial algebra over $R$.

\textup{(b)} If $B$ is an $R$-algebra of finite type, then we may take $P$ to be of finite type over $R$ so that $P \otimes_R \cdots \otimes_R P$ are all $R$-algebras of finite type. If $G$ is of finite type, then $G(P \otimes_R \cdots \otimes_R P)$ are all $A$-modules of finite type, so that $\check{H}^n(B/R, G)$ is an $A$-module of finite type since $A$ is noetherian.

\textup{(c)} If $0 \to F' \to F \to F'' \to 0$ is an exact sequence in $\underline{C}_{A|R}^{\sim}$, then
\[
0 \to F'(B) \to F(B) \to F''(B) \to \check{H}^1(B|R, F') \to \check{H}^1(B|R, F) \to \cdots
\]
is exact. If $P \in \Ob(\underline{C}_{A|R})$ is a polynomial algebra over $R$, then $\check{H}^1(P|R, F') = 0$ by 3.3(b) and hence $0 \to F'(P) \to F(P) \to F''(P) \to 0$ is exact. As for the other direction, let $\underline{P}_{A|R}$ be the full subcategory of $\underline{C}_{A|R}$ in which
\[
\Ob(\underline{P}_{A|R}) = \{P \in \Ob(\underline{C}_{A|R}) \mid P \text{ is a polynomial algebra over } R\}\;,
\]
provided with the induced topology, and let $p : \underline{P}_{A|R} \to \underline{C}_{A|R}$ be the inclusion functor. Since every object in $\underline{C}_{A|R}$ can be covered by an object in $\underline{P}_{A|R}$, it follows from a comparison lemma that $\widetilde{p} : \widetilde{\underline{C}}_{A|R} \to \widetilde{\underline{P}}_{A|R}$ is an equivalence of categories. Consequently, if $0 \to F'(P) \to F(P) \to F''(P) \to 0$ is exact for every $P \in \Ob(\underline{P}_{A|R})$, then $0 \to \widetilde{p}F' \to \widetilde{p}F \to \widetilde{p}F'' \to 0$ is exact in $\widetilde{\underline{P}}_{A|R}$ and hence $0 \to F' \to F \to F'' \to 0$ is exact in $\widetilde{\underline{C}}_{A|R}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
\textup{(a)} If $F$ is an additive sheaf on $\underline{C}_{R}$, then
\[
\check{H}^n(B/R, F) \simeq \check{H}^n(B/R, \widetilde{F})
\]
where $\widetilde{F}$ is the associated sheaf.

\textup{(b)} Let $R \to S$ be a map in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$. For any sheaf $F$ on $\underline{C}_{S}$, we have a spectral sequence
\[
E_2^{p,q} = \check{H}^p(B/R, \check{H}^q(F)) \Longrightarrow \check{H}^{p+q}(B/S, F)\;.
\]

\textup{(c)} (communicated from Rinehart) Consider the forgetful functor $\beta : \underline{C}_{A|R} \to \underline{C}_{A|S}$. We have a spectral sequence
\[
E_2^{p,q} = \check{H}^p(A|R, \beta^{\#}\check{H}^q_{A|S}(F)) \Longrightarrow \check{H}^{p+q}(A|S, F)
\]
where $\beta^{\#} = \underline{a}_{A|R} \circ \breve{\beta}$, $\underline{a}_{A|R} : \breve{\underline{C}}_{A|R} \to \widetilde{\underline{C}}_{A|R}$ is ``associated-sheaf'' functor. Now let $F$ be an additive sheaf. We claim that $\beta^{\#}\check{H}^q_{A|S}(F)$ is a constant sheaf for all $q > 0$. Indeed, if $P$ is a polynomial algebra over $R$, then $P = R \otimes_S P'$ for a polynomial algebra $P'$ over $S$ and hence
\[
[\beta^{\#}\check{H}^q_{A|S}(F)](P) = \check{H}^q(P|S, F) = \check{H}^q(R \otimes_S P'|S, F) \xrightarrow{\sim} \check{H}^q(R|S, F)
\]
is an isomorphism for all $q > 0$ by 3.7(b), and surjective for $q = 0$. It follows from 3.3(c) that the canonical map $\beta^{\#}\check{H}^q_{A|S}(F) \to \check{H}^q(R|S, F) = \text{constant sheaf}$ is an isomorphism for all $q > 0$, and is surjective for $q = 0$. It follows that $E_2^{p,q} = \check{H}^p(A|R, \beta^{\#}\check{H}^q_{A|S}(F)) = 0$ for all $p, q > 0$, and hence the above spectral sequence yields the exact sequence
\[
(*) \quad 0 \to E^{1,0} \to H^1 \to E^{0,1} \to E^{2,0} \to H^2 \to E^{0,2} \to E^{3,0} \to H^3 \to \cdots
\]
where we note that $E^{0,q} = [\beta^{\#}\check{H}^q_{A|S}(F)](A) \xrightarrow{\sim} \check{H}^q(R|S, F)$ for all $q > 0$ and $E^{p,0} = \check{H}^p(A|R, \widetilde{\beta}F)$. On the other hand, the exact sequence $0 \to F_R \to \widetilde{\beta}F \to F(R) \to 0$ gives us the exact sequence
\[
(**) \quad 0 \to \check{H}^0(A|R, F_R) \to \check{H}^0(A|R, \widetilde{\beta}F) \to \check{H}^0(A|R, F(R)) \to \check{H}^1(A|R, F_R) \to \check{H}^1(A|R, \widetilde{\beta}F) \to 0
\]
with $\check{H}^0(A|R, F_R) = \check{H}^0(A|S, F)$ and $\check{H}^0(A|R, F(R)) = \check{H}^0(R|S, F)$. The exact sequences $(*)$ and $(**)$ combined gives the desired exact sequence.
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
For an $R$-algebra $A$ in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ and an $A$-module $M$, we define
\[
D^i(A/R, M) = \check{H}^i(A/R, \Der_R(A, M))\;.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
\textup{(a)} $D^i(A/R, M)$ is an $A$-module.

\textup{(b)} $D^i(A/R, M) = 0$ for all $i > 0$ if $A$ is a polynomial algebra over $R$.

\textup{(c)} If $0 \to M' \to M \to M'' \to 0$ is an exact sequence of $A$-modules, then there is a long exact sequence
\begin{align*}
0 &\to \Der_R(A, M') \to \Der_R(A, M) \to \Der_R(A, M'') \\
&\to D^1(A/R, M') \to D^1(A/R, M) \to D^1(A/R, M'') \to \cdots\;.
\end{align*}

\textup{(d)} $D^i(A/R, M)$ does not depend on the base ring $\Lambda$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}
Let $A$ be an $R$-algebra of finite type and $M$ an $A$-module of finite type. Then $D^i(A/R, M)$ is an $A$-module of finite type.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proposition}
Let $R \to S$ be a map in $\underline{C}_{\Lambda}$ and $A$ an $S$-algebra. For any $A$-module $M$, we have a spectral sequence
\[
E_2^{p,q} = D^p(A/R, D^q(A/S, M)) \Longrightarrow D^{p+q}(A/S, M)\;.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}
\textup{(a)} $D^1(A/R, M) \simeq \Exalcomm(A/R, M)$, the module of commutative algebra extensions of $A$ by $M$ over $R$.

\textup{(b)} Let $A$ be a local $R$-algebra of essentially finite type. Then $D^2(A/R, M) = 0$ for all $A$-modules $M$ if and only if $A$ is a relative complete intersection over $R$.

\textup{(c)} Let $R$ be noetherian and $A$ a local $R$-algebra of essentially finite type. We denote by $\widehat{A}$ the $\fm$-adic completion of $A$ where $\fm = $ the maximal ideal of $A$. Then for any $A$-module $E$ of finite type we have a canonical isomorphism
\[
D^1(\widehat{A}/R, \widehat{A} \otimes_A E) \simeq \widehat{A} \otimes_A D^1(A/R, E)\;.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(a) follows immediately from $D^1(A/R, M) = \Exalcomm(A/R, M)$.

(b) Let $P$ be a polynomial algebra over $R$ in a finite number of variables and let $0 \to I \to P \to A \to 0$ be exact. Localizing if necessary, we may assume that $I$ is generated by a $P$-regular sequence which would imply that $H_1(K_I) = 0$ and therefore $D^2(A/R, M) = \Coker(H^1(K_I, M) \to \Hom_A(H_1(K_I), M)) = 0$. Conversely assume that $D^2(A/R, M) = 0$ for every $A$-module $M$.

Let $0 \to I \to P \to A \to 0$ be exact where $P$ is a polynomial algebra over $R$ in a finite number of variables. We have
\[
0 = D^2(A/R, M) = \Coker(H^1(K_I, M) \to \Hom_A(H_1(K_I), M))
\]
i.e., $H^1(K_I, M) \to \Hom_A(H_1(K_I), M)$ is surjective for all $A$-module $M$. Choose an exact sequence $0 \to K \to F \to I \to 0$ where $F$ is a free $P$-module of finite type. We note that $H_1(K_I) = K/K_0$ where $K_0 = \Im(\bigwedge^2 F \to \bigwedge^1 F)$. The fact that $D^2(A/R, H_1(K_I)) = 0$ i.e.,
\[
H^1(K_I, H_1(K_I)) \to \Hom_A(H_1(K_I), H_1(K_I)) \text{ is surjective}
\]
entails that $0 \to K/K_0 \to F/IF \to I/I^2 \to 0$ is exact and splits as $A$-modules and in particular $I/I^2$ is projective as $A$-module. Therefore, localizing $A$ if necessary, we may assume that $I/I^2$ is $A$-free, and that $F/IF \to I/I^2$ is an isomorphism. Then the fact that $0 \to K/K_0 \to F/IF \to I/I^2 \to 0$ is exact and splits as $A$-modules entails that $H_1(K_I) = K/K_0 = 0$. This means that $I$ is generated by a $P$-regular sequence i.e., $A$ is a relative complete intersection over $R$.

(c) follows immediately from 3.12(4) and (6).

(d) Firstly it follows from 3.12(6) that
\[
D^1(A/R, \widehat{A} \otimes E) \simeq \widehat{A} \otimes_A D^1(A/R, E)
\]
is an isomorphism for all $i$, and therefore it suffices to show that $D^1(\widehat{A}/R, \widehat{A} \otimes_A E) \simeq \widehat{A} \otimes_A D^1(A/R, E)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{8. Deformations of schemes}

Let $k$ be a field and $\underline{C}_k$ the category of artinian local $k$-algebras with residue field $k$. Let $X_0$ be a scheme of finite type over $k$. By a \emph{deformation} of $X_0$ to $R \in \underline{C}_k$, we mean a pair $(R, X)$ where $X$ is a scheme flat over $R$ together with a morphism $X_0 \to X$ over $R$ such that the induced morphism $X_0 \to X \times_{\Spec(R)} \Spec(k)$ is an isomorphism. We note that, since $R$ is an artinian local ring, $i_X : X_0 \to X$ is necessarily a closed immersion which is a homeomorphism on underlying topological spaces. If $(R, X)$, $(R', X')$ are deformations of $X_0$ to $R$, $R'$ respectively, we define an arrow $(R', X') \to (R, X)$ to consist of a map $R' \xrightarrow{\varphi} R$ in $\underline{C}_k$ and a morphism $\Phi : X \to X'$ of schemes with a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
X & & \xrightarrow{\Phi} & & X' \\
\downarrow & \searrow & & \swarrow & \downarrow \\
& X_0 & & & \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow & & \downarrow \\
\Spec(R) & & \xrightarrow{\Spec(\varphi)} & & \Spec(R')
\end{array}
\]
We thus obtain the category $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ of (infinitesimal) deformations of $X_0$ in which the objects and the arrows are defined as above. The category $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is provided with a functor $p : \underline{M}_{X_0} \to \underline{C}_k$ defined by $(R, X) \mapsto R$. Now let $(R, X) \in \Ob(\underline{M}_{X_0})$ and a map $R \xrightarrow{\varphi} S$ in $\underline{C}_k$ be given. We then obtain a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
X \times_{\Spec(R)} \Spec(S) & \xrightarrow{p_1} & X \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
\Spec(S) & \xrightarrow{\Spec(\varphi)} & \Spec(R)
\end{array}
\]
and the arrow $(\varphi, p_1) : (R, X) \to (S, X \times_{\Spec(R)} \Spec(S))$ is obviously cocartesian in $\underline{M}_{X_0}$. It follows that the category $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is a cofibered category over $\underline{C}_k$ via $p : \underline{M}_{X_0} \to \underline{C}_k$.

We observe the following facts:

\begin{proposition}
\textup{(a)} $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is a homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_k$.

\textup{(b)} If $X_0$ is proper over $k$ (resp.\ affine), then for any deformation $(R, X)$ of $X_0$, $X$ is proper (resp.\ affine) over $R$.

\textup{(c)} If $X_0$ is proper over $k$, then $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is semi-homogeneous.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
Assume that $X_0$ is proper over $k$. Then
\[
[\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\varepsilon])] \simeq H^1(X_0, \cT_{X_0})
\]
where $\cT_{X_0}$ is the tangent sheaf of $X_0$ over $k$, and
\[
\Aut(k[\varepsilon]) \simeq H^0(X_0, \cT_{X_0})\;.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}
Let $X_0$ be a proper scheme over $k$ such that $H^1(X_0, \cT_{X_0})$ and $H^2(X_0, \cT_{X_0})$ are finite-dimensional $k$-vector spaces. Then $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is minimally pro-representable.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}
If $X_0$ is a non-singular proper scheme over $k$, then $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is minimally pro-representable.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proposition}
Let $X_0$ be a proper scheme over $k$ and $Z_0$ a closed subscheme of $X_0$. Let $\underline{M}_{X_0, Z_0}$ be the category of deformations of the pair $(X_0, Z_0)$. Then $\underline{M}_{X_0, Z_0}$ is a homogeneous cofibered groupoid over $\underline{C}_k$. If $X_0$ and $Z_0$ are proper over $k$, then $\underline{M}_{X_0, Z_0}$ is minimally pro-representable, provided $H^1(X_0, \cT_{X_0}(-Z_0))$ and $H^2(X_0, \cT_{X_0}(-Z_0))$ are finite-dimensional.
\end{proposition}

\subsection*{9. Deformations of schemes (continued)}

\noindent\textbf{4.1(a):} Let $(1_R,\Phi) : (R, X_1) \to (R, X_2)$ be an arrow in $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ i.e., $\Phi : X_2 \to X_1$ is a morphism over $R$ such that $X_2 \otimes_R k \to X_1 \otimes_R k$ is an isomorphism. Since $R$ is artinian and $X_i$ is $R$-flat, it follows immediately that $\Phi$ is an isomorphism so that $(1_R,\Phi) : (R, X_1) \to (R, X_2)$ is an isomorphism in $\underline{M}_{X_0}$. Consequently, $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is a cofibered category in groupoids over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.1(b):} Suppose that $X_0$ is affine, say $X_0 = \Spec(A_0)$. Then for any object $(R, X) \in \ob(\underline{M}_{X_0})$, $X$ is also affine. Consequently, for any $R \in \ob(\underline{C}_\Lambda)$, the category $\underline{M}_{X_0}(R)$ is canonically equivalent, via the sections, to the category in which an object is a flat algebra $A$ over $R$ together with an $R$-algebra map $j_A : A \to A_0$ inducing an isomorphism $A \otimes_R k \xrightarrow{\sim} A_0$, and a map $\Phi : A_1 \to A_2$ is an $R$-algebra map with a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
A_1 & \xrightarrow{\Phi} & A_2 \\
\scriptstyle{j_{A_1}}\downarrow & & \downarrow\scriptstyle{j_{A_2}} \\
A_0 & \longrightarrow & A_0
\end{array}
\]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.1(c):} Consider the cofibered category $\widehat{\underline{M}}_{X_0}$ in groupoids over $\widehat{\underline{C}}_\Lambda$. By definition, the category $\widehat{\underline{M}}_{X_0}(R)$ for any $R \in \ob(\widehat{\underline{C}}_\Lambda)$ is canonically equivalent to the category of formal schemes $\mathfrak{X}$, adic over the formal spectrum $\Spf(R)$, such that $\mathfrak{X}_n = \mathfrak{X} \otimes_R R/\underline{m}^{n+1}$ is flat over $R/\underline{m}^{n+1}$ for every $n \geq 0$ (or what amounts to the same, if $X_0$ is noetherian, such that $\mathfrak{X}$ is flat over $\Spf(R)$). Consequently, if $X_0$ is noetherian, $\widehat{\underline{M}}_{X_0}$ is canonically $\widehat{\underline{C}}_\Lambda$-equivalent to the category of deformations of $X_0$ over the objects in $\widehat{\underline{C}}_\Lambda$, in the sense of formal schemes.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.1(d):} Let $(R, X) \in \ob(\underline{M}_{X_0})$ i.e., we have a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
X_0 & \xrightarrow{i_X} & X \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow\scriptstyle{\text{flat}} \\
\Spec(k) & \longrightarrow & \Spec(R)
\end{array}
\]
such that $X_0 \to X \times_{\Spec(R)} \Spec(k)$ is an isomorphism. Now let $U_0 \subset X_0$ be a fixed open subscheme of $X_0$. Since $i_X : X_0 \to X$ is a closed immersion which is a homeomorphism on the underlying topological spaces, we obtain a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
U_0 & \xrightarrow{i_X|_{U_0}} & X|_{U_0} \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow\scriptstyle{\text{flat}} \\
\Spec(k) & \longrightarrow & \Spec(R)
\end{array}
\]
such that $U_0 \to (X|_{U_0}) \times_{\Spec(R)} \Spec(k) = X \otimes_R k|_{U_0}$ is an isomorphism, so that $(R, X|_{U_0})$ is a deformation of $U_0$ to $R$. Furthermore, for any arrow $(\varphi,\Phi) : (R, X) \to (R', X')$ in $\underline{M}_{X_0}$, $(\varphi, \Phi|_{U_0}) : (R, X|_{U_0}) \to (R', X'|_{U_0})$ is an arrow in $\underline{M}_{U_0}$. Consequently we have the restriction functor $\underline{M}_{X_0} \to \underline{M}_{U_0}$ over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$.

One may also consider a localization as well as a formalization at a point. Namely let $x_0$ be a fixed point in $X_0$. If $(R, X) \in \ob(\underline{M}_{X_0})$, then $\Spec(\underline{O}_{X,x_0})$ is a deformation of $\Spec(\underline{O}_{X_0,x_0})$ to $R$ and $\Spec(\widehat{\underline{O}}_{X,x_0})$ is a deformation of $\Spec(\widehat{\underline{O}}_{X_0,x_0})$ over $R$, where $\widehat{\quad}$ stands for the completion with respect to the linear topology given by the powers of the maximal ideal. We thus obtain functors $\underline{M}_{X_0} \to \underline{M}_{\Spec(\underline{O}_{X_0,x_0})} \to \underline{M}_{\Spec(\widehat{\underline{O}}_{X_0,x_0})}$.

Firstly we establish that $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is a homogeneous cofibered category over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Lemma 4.2.} \textit{Consider a diagram}
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
& & R' \\
& & \downarrow \\
R & \xrightarrow{\text{surjective}} & \bar{R}
\end{array}
\]
\textit{in $\underline{C}_\Lambda$, and let $E$, $E'$ be flat modules over $R$, $R'$ respectively. Then for any $R'$-homomorphism $E' \to \bar{E} = E \otimes_R \bar{R}$, the fibre-product $E \times_{\bar{E}} E'$ is flat as $R \times_{\bar{R}} R'$-module.}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} Let $0 \to I \to R \to \bar{R} \to 0$ be exact so that $0 \to I \to R \times_{\bar{R}} R' \to R' \to 0$ is exact. Since $E$ is $R$-flat, $0 \to I \otimes_R E \to E \to \bar{E} \to 0$ is exact and hence we obtain the exact sequence
\[(*) \qquad 0 \to I \otimes_R E \to E \times_{\bar{E}} E' \to E' \to 0.\]
Set $S = R \times_{\bar{R}} R$. Since $I$ is nilpotent, $E \times_{\bar{E}} E'$ is flat as an $S$-module if and only if $(E \times_{\bar{E}} E') \otimes_S R'$ is flat as an $R'$-module and $\Tor_1^S(E \times_{\bar{E}} E', R') = 0$.

However, we note that $I \otimes_R E = I \otimes_S (E \times_{\bar{E}} E')$ and $E' = R' \otimes_S (E \times_{\bar{E}} E')$ and therefore the exact sequence $(*)$ can be rewritten as
\[0 \to I \otimes_S (E \times_{\bar{E}} E') \to E \times_{\bar{E}} E' \to R' \otimes_S (E \times_{\bar{E}} E') \to 0.\]
This means that $\Tor_1^S(R', E \times_{\bar{E}} E') = 0$ and $R' \otimes_S (E \times_{\bar{E}} E') = E'$ is flat as $R'$-module, and therefore $E \times_{\bar{E}} E'$ is $S$-flat.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Corollary 4.3.} \textit{Given a diagram}
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
(R, X) & & & & (R', X') \\
& \scriptstyle{(\varphi,\Phi)}\searrow & & \swarrow\scriptstyle{(\varphi',\Phi')} & \\
& & (\bar{R}, \bar{X}) & &
\end{array}
\]
\textit{in $\underline{M}_{X_0}$, the amalgamated sum $X \amalg_{\bar{X}} X'$ (in the sense of schemes) is a flat scheme over $R \times_{\bar{R}} R'$, provided $\varphi : R \to \bar{R}$ is surjective. Consequently, $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is a homogeneous cofibered category in groupoids over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$.}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} For each affine open $U_0 \subset X_0$, we have by definition that $X \amalg_{\bar{X}} X'|_{U_0} \simeq \Spec(\Gamma(U_0, X) \times_{\Gamma(U_0,\bar{X})} \Gamma(U_0, X'))$. Consequently, if $\varphi : R \to \bar{R}$ is surjective, $X \amalg_{\bar{X}} X'$ is a flat scheme over $R \times_{\bar{R}} R'$ by 4.2, and the morphism $i_X \amalg_{i_{\bar{X}}} i_{X'} : X_0 \to X \amalg_{\bar{X}} X'$ induces an isomorphism $X_0 \to (X \amalg_{\bar{X}} X') \otimes_S k$, where $S = R \times_{\bar{R}} R'$. Therefore $(R \times_{\bar{R}} R', X \amalg_{\bar{X}} X') \in \ob(\underline{M}_{X_0})$ and we obviously have that $(R \times_{\bar{R}} R', X \amalg_{\bar{X}} X') = (R, X) \times_{(\bar{R},\bar{X})} (R', X')$ in the category $\underline{M}_{X_0}$, which means that $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is homogeneous over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Lemma 4.4.} \textit{For any scheme $X_0$ over $k$, we have a canonical exact sequence}
\[0 \to H^1(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0) \to [\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])] \to H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)\]
\textit{where $\underline{D}_{X_0}^i$ are as defined in paragraph 3.}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} (Special case) Assume that $X_0$ is affine and let $\Gamma(X_0, \underline{O}_{X_0}) = A_0$. Then as it was noted in 4.1(b), the category $\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])$ is canonically equivalent to the category $\underline{E}$, where

$\ob(\underline{E})$: object in $\underline{E}$ is a flat algebra $A$ over $k[\epsilon]$

together with a $k[\epsilon]$-algebra map $j_A : A \to A_0$ inducing the isomorphism $k \otimes_{k[\epsilon]} j_A : k \otimes_{k[\epsilon]} A \xrightarrow{\sim} A_0$. Alternatively, an object in $\underline{E}$ is a $k$-algebra $A$ together with an exact sequence $0 \to A_0 \xrightarrow{\epsilon} A \xrightarrow{j_A} A_0 \to 0$ in which $\epsilon(A_0)^2 = 0$ as an ideal in $A$.

An arrow $A_1 \to A_2$ in $\underline{E}$ is a $k[\epsilon]$-algebra map $\Phi : A_1 \to A_2$ such that $j_{A_1} = j_{A_2} \circ \Phi$. Therefore, an arrow in the alternative description is a $k$-algebra map $\Phi : A_1 \to A_2$ yielding a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccccccccc}
0 & \longrightarrow & A_0 & \xrightarrow{\epsilon} & A_1 & \xrightarrow{j_{A_1}} & A_0 & \longrightarrow & 0 \\
& & \parallel & & \downarrow\scriptstyle{\Phi} & & \parallel & & \\
0 & \longrightarrow & A_0 & \xrightarrow{\epsilon} & A_2 & \xrightarrow{j_{A_2}} & A_0 & \longrightarrow & 0
\end{array}
\]
It follows that $[\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])]$ is canonically isomorphic, via $\Gamma(X_0, -)$, to the set of isomorphism classes of $k$-algebra extensions of $A_0$ by $A_0$. In other words, we have a canonical bijection $[\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])] \xrightarrow{\sim} H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)$ via $\Gamma(X_0, -)$, which is trivially seen to respect the vector space structures on both sides.

(General case) For each affine open $U_0 \subset X_0$, we get a canonical map $[\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])] \to H^0(U_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)$ defined as the composite map $[\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])] \to [\underline{M}_{U_0}(k[\epsilon])] \xrightarrow{\sim} H^0(U_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)$ and hence we obtain a canonical map $[\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])] \to H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)$. Its kernel is the set of isomorphism classes of locally-trivial deformations of $X_0$ to $k[\epsilon]$, i.e., the set of isomorphism classes of deformations of $X_0$ to $k[\epsilon]$, locally (on $X$) isomorphic to $X_0 \otimes_k k[\epsilon]$. Since $\underline{D}_{X_0}^0 = \underline{\Der}_k(\underline{O}_{X_0}, \underline{O}_{X_0}) = \underline{\Aut}(X_0 \otimes_k k[\epsilon]|_{X_0}) = {}$ the sheaf of germs of automorphisms of $X_0 \otimes_k k[\epsilon]$ inducing the identity on the subscheme $X_0$, it is canonically isomorphic to $H^1(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0)$, so that we obtain the desired exact sequence.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Theorem 4.5.} \textit{Let $X_0$ be a scheme of finite type over $k$ for which either}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] \textit{$X_0$ is proper over $k$ or}
\item[(ii)] \textit{$X_0$ is affine but $X_0^{\mathrm{sing}} = \{x \in X_0 \mid X_0$ is non-smooth over $k$ at the point $x\}$ is a finite set.}
\end{itemize}
\textit{Then}

\textit{(a) $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ admits a formal versal object (called a formal versal deformation of $X_0$) over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$. If $(R, \mathfrak{X})$ is a formal versal deformation of $X_0$, then for any $(S, \mathfrak{Y}) \in \ob(\widehat{\underline{M}}_{X_0})$, there is a map $(\varphi,\Phi) : (R,\mathfrak{X}) \to (S,\mathfrak{Y})$ in $\widehat{\underline{M}}_{X_0}$ such that the diagram below is cartesian}
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
\mathfrak{Y} & \xrightarrow{\Phi} & \mathfrak{X} \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow \\
\Spf(S) & \xrightarrow{\Spf(\varphi)} & \Spf(R)
\end{array}
\]

\textit{Furthermore a formal versal deformation $(R,\mathfrak{X})$ of $X_0$ prorepresents the functor $[\underline{M}_{X_0}]$ if and only if, for every surjective map $R' \to R$ in $\underline{C}_\Lambda$ and a deformation $(R', X')$ of $X_0$ to $R'$, the homomorphism}
\[\Aut_{R'}(X'|_{X_0}) \to \Aut_R(X' \otimes_R R|_{X_0})\]
\textit{is surjective.}

\textit{(b) If $X_0$ is proper over $k$, then $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is smoothly pseudo-representable over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$.}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} (a) Since $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is a homogeneous cofibered category in groupoids over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$ by 4.3, it suffices to see that $[\underline{M}(k[\epsilon])]$ is a finite-dimensional vector space over $k$ by 1.11 and 2.9(b).

Now consider the exact sequence
\[(*) \qquad 0 \to H^1(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0) \to [\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])] \to H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1).\]
If $X_0$ is proper over $k$, then $H^1(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0)$ and $H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)$ are both finite dimensional since $\underline{D}_{X_0}^i$ are coherent for all $i \geq 0$ by 2.13(a). Now assume that $X_0$ is affine such that $X_0^{\mathrm{sing}}$ is a finite set. Since $\underline{D}_{X_0}^1$ is a coherent sheaf on $X_0$ such that $\supp \underline{D}_{X_0}^1 \subset X_0^{\mathrm{sing}}$ by 2.13(a), we find that $H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)$ is a finite-dimensional vector space over $k$, whereas $H^1(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0) = 0$ since $X_0$ is affine. Therefore, in either case, it follows from the exact sequence $(*)$ that $[\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])]$ is a finite dimensional vector space over $k$. The second statement of (a) follows from 2.7.

(b) If $X_0$ is proper, $\Aut(X_0 \otimes_k k[\epsilon]|_{X_0}) = H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0)$ is a finite dimensional vector space and hence our statement follows from 2.13.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Remark 4.6(a):} Let $X_0$ be affine. Then a formal versal deformation of $X_0$ exists if and only if $\underline{D}_{X_0}^1$ has its support on a finite number of points. However this does not imply that non-smooth points of $X_0$ are all isolated. There are affine schemes $X_0$ over $k$ having a non-isolated non-smooth point but $\supp \underline{D}_{X_0}^1$ is a finite set.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Remark 4.6(b):} Henceforth a deformation of $X_0$ is meant by an object in $\underline{M}_{X_0}$. In case when there is a possible confusion, an object in $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ will be referred as an infinitesimal deformation of $X_0$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Remark 4.6(c):} Let $X_0$ be a scheme over $k$. We have fixed a complete local noetherian ring $\Lambda$ with the residue field $k$, and considered the deformations of $X_0$ over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$. However, if we drop the condition that a deformation is flat, everything which has been said in this paragraph remains valid. The condition of flatness was needed only for the existence of a formal versal deformation.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.7.} Let $X_0$ be a scheme of finite type over $k$. We define a functor $\underline{M}_{X_0}^{\mathrm{loc}}$ from $\underline{C}_\Lambda$ to (Sets) as follows: For $R \in \ob(\underline{C}_\Lambda)$, $\underline{M}_{X_0}^{\mathrm{loc}}(R)$ is the set of isomorphism classes of deformations of $X_0$ to $R$ which are locally isomorphic to $X_0 \otimes_k R$. Similarly we define $\widehat{\underline{M}}_{X_0}^{\mathrm{loc}}$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Theorem 4.8.} \textit{Let $X_0$ be a scheme of finite type over $k$ such that $H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0) = 0$. Then}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(a)] \textit{$\underline{M}_{X_0}^{\mathrm{loc}}$ is strictly prorepresentable.}
\item[(b)] \textit{If either $X_0$ is proper over $k$ or $X_0$ is affine and $X_0^{\mathrm{sing}}$ is a finite set, then $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is strictly prorepresentable.}
\end{itemize}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} (a) Since $H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0) = 0$, there are no non-trivial automorphisms for any deformation of $X_0$, and hence $\underline{M}_{X_0}^{\mathrm{loc}}$ is equivalent to a functor from $\underline{C}_\Lambda$ to (Sets). By 4.4, $[\underline{M}_{X_0}^{\mathrm{loc}}(k[\epsilon])] = H^1(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0)$ is a finite-dimensional vector space over $k$ (under the assumption of properness or isolated singularities). The prorepresentability then follows from Schlessinger's criterion.

(b) follows from (a) and the fact that $H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0) = 0$ implies the condition in 4.5(a) for prorepresentability.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.9.} We now consider the exact sequence from 4.4 more closely. Let
\[0 \to H^1(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0) \to [\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])] \to H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)\]
be the exact sequence. The map $[\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])] \to H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)$ is the obstruction map. Its image consists of those obstruction classes which vanish.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.10.} \textit{Let $X_0$ be an affine scheme of finite type over $k$.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(a)] \textit{If $X_0$ is locally a complete-intersection, then $\underline{M}_{X_0}$ is smooth over $\underline{C}_\Lambda$.}
\item[(b)] \textit{Assume that, for a fixed closed point $x \in X_0$, $X_0 - \{x\}$ is smooth over $k$. Then}
\[\underline{M}_{X_0} \to \underline{M}_{\Spec(\underline{O}_{X_0,x})} \to \underline{M}_{\Spec(\widehat{\underline{O}}_{X_0,x})}\]
\textit{are both minimally smooth functors. In particular, if $(R, \mathfrak{X})$ is a formal versal deformation of $X_0$, then $(R, \mathfrak{X}_{(x)})$ and $(R, \mathfrak{X}_{(\widehat{x})})$ are formal versal deformations of $\Spec(\underline{O}_{X_0,x})$ and $\Spec(\widehat{\underline{O}}_{X_0,x})$ respectively.}
\end{itemize}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} In this proof we go back to the notations of the cohomology of commutative algebras defined in the paragraph 3. We set $X_0 = \Spec(A_0)$.

(a) If $X_0$ is locally a complete-intersection, then the cotangent complex has certain vanishing properties which guarantee smoothness of the deformation functor. In particular, $D^2(A_0/k, A_0) = 0$, which implies that the deformation functor is unobstructed, hence smooth.

(b) follows from 4.10 and 4.9(a).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.14.} We close this paragraph with an explicit construction of a formal versal deformation in a simple situation. In fact, if $X_0$ is affine and is a complete-intersection, it is trivial to write down explicitely. Thus let
\[A_0 = k[x_1, \ldots, x_n]/(f_1, \ldots, f_r) \quad S/J\]
where $S = k[x_1, \ldots, x_n]$, $J = (f_1, \ldots, f_r)$, and $f_1, \ldots, f_r$ is an $S$-regular sequence. We then obtain the exact sequence
\[\cdots \to \Der_k(S, A_0) \to \Hom_{A_0}(J/J^2, A_0) \to D^1(A_0/k, A_0) \to 0\]
We note that $J/J^2$ is $A_0$-free module of rank $r$ generated by $f_1 (\mod J^2), \ldots, f_r (\mod J^2)$, since $f_1, \ldots, f_r$ is an $S$-regular sequence. We now assume that $D^1(A_0/k, A_0)$ is a finite-dimensional vector space over $k$, say $A$ has isolated non-smooth points only. Let $d = \dim_k D^1(A_0/k, A_0)$ and choose $p_j : J/J^2 \to A_0$ $(j = 1, 2, \ldots, d)$ which form a $k$-basis of $D^1(A_0/k, A_0)$. Now choose $P_{jk} \in \Lambda[x_1, \ldots, x_n]$ such that $p_j(f_i) = P_{ji} \pmod{\underline{m}_\Lambda + J}$ where $\underline{m}_\Lambda$ is the maximal ideal of $\Lambda$. We then consider
\begin{align*}
R &= \Lambda[[t_1, \ldots, t_d]] \\
B &= R[x_1, \ldots, x_n]/(G_1, \ldots, G_r)
\end{align*}
where $G_j = F_j - \sum\limits_{i=1}^d t_i P_{ji}$ and $F_j$ is a polynomial with coefficients in $\Lambda$ gotten from $f_j$ by lifting the coefficients from $k$ to $\Lambda$. Since $R[x_1, \ldots, x_n]$ is $R$-flat and $G_1, \ldots, G_r$ modulo $\underline{m}_R$ (which becomes $f_1, \ldots, f_r$) is a regular sequence in $k \otimes_R R[x_1, \ldots, x_n]$, it follows that $B$ is $R$-flat, and $k \otimes_R B = A_0$. Furthermore,
\[\Hom_{\Lambda\text{-alg}}(R, k[\epsilon]) \to D^1(A_0/k, A_0) = [\underline{M}_{\Spec(A_0)}(k[\epsilon])]\]
is bijective by our construction of $B$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{5. Fitting ideals}

\medskip
We now globalize the definition of Fitting ideals. Thus let $X$ be any scheme, and $\underline{E}$ a quasi-coherent $\mathcal{O}_X$-module of finite presentation. We then obtain a sequence of quasi-coherent ideal sheaves $\underline{I}_X^p(\underline{E})$ for $p \geq 0$ defined by
\[\Gamma(U, \underline{I}_X^p(\underline{E})) \simeq I_{\Gamma(U,\mathcal{O}_X)}^p(\Gamma(U, \underline{E}))\]
for every affine open subset $U \subset X$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Proposition 5.1.} \textit{The Fitting ideals satisfy the following properties:}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(a)] \textit{$\underline{I}_X^0(\underline{E}) \subset \underline{I}_X^1(\underline{E}) \subset \cdots$}
\item[(b)] \textit{If $0 \to \underline{E}' \to \underline{E} \to \underline{E}'' \to 0$ is an exact sequence of quasi-coherent $\mathcal{O}_X$-modules of finite presentation, then}
\[\underline{I}_X^p(\underline{E}) \supset \sum_{r+s=p} \underline{I}_X^r(\underline{E}') \cdot \underline{I}_X^s(\underline{E}'').\]
\item[(c)] \textit{If $\underline{E}$ is locally free of rank $r$, then $\underline{I}_X^p(\underline{E}) = \mathcal{O}_X$ for $p \geq r$ and the zero sheaf for $p < r$.}
\item[(d)] \textit{For any morphism $f : Y \to X$,}
\[\underline{I}_Y^p(f^*\underline{E}) \supset f^*\underline{I}_X^p(\underline{E})\cdot \mathcal{O}_Y\]
\textit{with equality if $f$ is flat.}
\end{itemize}

\medskip
Now let $X \to Y$ be a morphism of schemes. The relative K"ahler-differentials $\Omega_{X/Y}^1 (= \underline{\Omega}_{X|Y}^1)$ is a quasi-coherent $\mathcal{O}_X$-module. If the morphism $X \to Y$ is of essentially finite presentation, then $\underline{\Omega}_{X|Y}^1$ is a quasi-coherent $\mathcal{O}_X$-module of finite presentation, and in turn we may consider the ideal sheaves $\underline{I}_X^p(\underline{\Omega}_{X|Y}^1)$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Definition 5.2.} \textit{Let $X \to Y$ be a morphism of essentially finite presentation. For each integer $p \geq 0$, we define $J_X^p(X|Y)$ to be the closed subscheme of $X$ defined by the ideal sheaf $\underline{I}_X^p(\underline{\Omega}_{X|Y}^1)$.}

\medskip
The closed subscheme $J_X^p(X|Y)$ is called the \textit{$p$-th Jacobian subscheme} of $X$ over $Y$. Its underlying set consists of points $x \in X$ where $\Omega_{X/Y}^1$ requires more than $p$ generators, i.e., points where the relative dimension (in the sense of minimal number of generators of $\Omega^1$) is $> p$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Proposition 5.3.} \textit{The Jacobian subschemes satisfy the following properties:}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] \textit{$J_X^0(X|Y) \subset J_X^1(X|Y) \subset J_X^2(X|Y) \subset \cdots$}
\item[(ii)] \textit{$J_X^p(X|Y) = X$ for $p \geq \dim(\Omega_{X/Y}^1 \otimes k(x))$ at all points $x$.}
\item[(iii)] \textit{If $X \to Y$ is smooth, then $J_X^p(X|Y) = \emptyset$ for all $p \geq \dim(X/Y)$.}
\item[(iv)] \textit{Formation of $J_X^p(X|Y)$ commutes with flat base change in $Y$.}
\end{itemize}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{5.4.} Let $S$ be a noetherian formal scheme, and $\mathfrak{X}$ an $S$-adic formal scheme of essentially finite type. We then define
\[I_{\mathfrak{X}}^p(\mathfrak{X}|S) = \varprojlim_n I_{X_n}^p(X_n|S_n)\]
\[J_{\mathfrak{X}}^p(\mathfrak{X}|S) = \varprojlim_n J_{X_n}^p(X_n|S_n)\]
where $X_n = \mathfrak{X} \times_{\Spf(R)} \Spec(R/\underline{m}^{n+1})$ and similarly for $S_n$.

$\mathfrak{X}_{(x)}$ is an $S$-adic formal scheme of essentially finite type and we have a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
\mathfrak{X}_{(\hat{x})} & \xrightarrow{i_{(\hat{x})}} & \mathfrak{X} \\
\downarrow & & \downarrow\scriptstyle{i_{(x)}} \\
\mathfrak{X}_{(x)} & &
\end{array}
\]
We note that if $x$ is an isolated point of $\mathfrak{X}$, then $i_{(x)}$, $i_{(\hat{x})}$ are both isomorphisms to the connected component of $\mathfrak{X}$ containing the point $x$.

In particular, if $|\mathfrak{X}| = \{x_1, \ldots, x_m\}$ is a finite discrete space, then we have a canonical identification $\mathfrak{X} = \coprod\limits_{1 \leq i \leq m} \mathfrak{X}_{(x_i)}$ (disjoint union).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Corollary 5.7.} \textit{Let $S$ be a noetherian formal scheme, and $\mathfrak{X}$ an $S$-adic formal scheme of essentially finite type. We then have}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] \textit{$J_{\mathfrak{X}}^p(\mathfrak{X}|S)$ commutes with base change i.e., $J_{\mathfrak{X}'}^p(\mathfrak{X}'|S') = J_{\mathfrak{X}}^p(\mathfrak{X}|S) \times_S S'$ where $\mathfrak{X}' = \mathfrak{X} \times_S S'$.}
\item[(2)] \textit{The connected components of $J_{\mathfrak{X}}^p(\mathfrak{X}|S)$ are in bijective correspondence with that of $J_{X_0}^p(X_0|S_0)$. For each point $x \in |J_{X_0}^p(X_0|S_0)|$ we have a canonical isomorphism $J_{\mathfrak{X}}^p(\mathfrak{X}|S)_{(x)} \simeq J_{\mathfrak{X}_{(x)}}^p(\mathfrak{X}_{(x)}|S)$. In particular if $|J_{X_0}^p(X_0|S_0)| = \{x_1, \ldots, x_m\}$ is discrete, then}
\[J_{\mathfrak{X}}^p(\mathfrak{X}|S) = \coprod_{1 \leq i \leq m} J_{\mathfrak{X}_{(x_i)}}^p(\mathfrak{X}_{(x_i)}|S)\]
\textit{(disjoint union).}
\end{itemize}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{6. Local complete intersections}

\medskip
We now specialize to the case of local complete intersections. The results of this section will be used in the study of deformations of curves with ordinary double points.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Proposition 6.1.} \textit{Let $A_0$ be a local $k$-algebra, essentially of finite type, such that}
\[\widehat{A}_0 \simeq k[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]/(f)\]
\textit{where $f$ is a non-degenerate quadratic form. Then $D^1(A_0/k, A_0)$ is a 1-dimensional vector space over $k$, and a formal versal deformation of $X_0 = \Spec(A_0)$ is given by}
\[R = \Lambda[[t]], \quad \widehat{A} = R[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]/(F - t)\]
\textit{where $F$ is a quadratic polynomial in $\Lambda[x_1, \ldots, x_n]$ obtained from $f$ by lifting the coefficients to $\Lambda$.}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} Since $f$ is a non-degenerate quadratic form, the module of K"ahler differentials $\Omega_{A_0/k}^1$ has a single relation given by the partial derivatives of $f$. The non-degeneracy implies that $D^1(A_0/k, A_0) \simeq k$. The explicit construction follows the method of 4.14 with $d = 1$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Lemma 6.2.} \textit{Let $A_0$ be a local $k$-algebra, essentially of finite type such that}
\[\widehat{A}_0 \simeq k[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]/(f)\]
\textit{where $f$ is a non-degenerate quadratic form, and let $(R, \mathfrak{X})$ be a formal versal deformation of $X_0 = \Spec(A_0)$. Then}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] \textit{$J_{\mathfrak{X}}(\mathfrak{X}|R) \to J_R(\mathfrak{X}|R) \to \Spec(\Lambda)$ are both isomorphisms.}
\item[(2)] \textit{$J_R(\mathfrak{X}|R)$ is a principal ideal in $R$ generated by a free formal generator of $R$ over $\Lambda$ i.e. $I_R(\mathfrak{X}|R) = (t)$ and $R = \Lambda[[t]]$.}
\item[(3)] \textit{$\mathfrak{X} \to \Spec(\Lambda)$ is formally smooth.}
\end{itemize}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} We set $\mathfrak{X} = \Spf(\widehat{A})$. By 4.10(b), $(R, \Spf(\widehat{A}))$ is a formal versal deformation of $\widehat{X}_0 = \Spec(\widehat{A}_0)$ where $\widehat{A}_0 = \varprojlim_v A \otimes_R R_v$.

Now $\widehat{A}_0 \simeq k[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]/(f)$ and $(\partial f/\partial x_1, \ldots, \partial f/\partial x_n) = (x_1, \ldots, x_n)$ in $k[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]$. It follows readily that $[\underline{M}_{X_0}(k[\epsilon])] \simeq k$ and, by 4.14, we get
\[R \simeq \Lambda[[t]] \quad \text{and} \quad \widehat{A} \simeq R[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]/(F - t)\]
where $F$ is a quadratic polynomial in $\Lambda[x_1, \ldots, x_n]$ ($\subset R[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]$) obtained from $f$ by lifting the coefficients to $\Lambda$. Now
\[0 \to \widehat{A} \xrightarrow{df} \widehat{A}dx_1 \oplus \cdots \oplus \widehat{A}dx_n \longrightarrow \widehat{\Omega}_{\widehat{A}|R}^1 \to 0\]
so that $\widehat{\Omega}_{\widehat{A}|R}^1$ is the quotient of the free module by the single relation $df = 0$. Since $f$ is non-degenerate, this relation is independent of the relations modulo $t$, and we find that the Fitting ideal $I_R(\mathfrak{X}|R) = (t)$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Proposition 6.3.} \textit{With the same notation as in 6.2, we have the exact sequence}
\[0 \to A_0 \xrightarrow{(\partial f/\partial x_1, \ldots, \partial f/\partial x_n)} A_0^n \to \Omega_{A_0/k}^1 \to 0\]
\textit{and $I_{A_0}^p(\Omega_{A_0/k}^1) = A_0$ for $p \geq n-1$, while $I_{A_0}^{n-2}(\Omega_{A_0/k}^1) = \underline{m}_{A_0}$ is the maximal ideal.}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Lemma 6.4.} \textit{Let $X_0$ be a $k$-scheme of finite type with isolated non-smooth points only. Denote by $\{z_1, \ldots, z_r\}$ the set of non-smooth points in $X_0$. Assume that each $z_i$ is an ordinary quadratic singularity (i.e., $\widehat{\underline{O}}_{X_0, z_i} \simeq k[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]/(q_i)$ where $q_i$ is a non-degenerate quadratic form). Then}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(a)] \textit{$R$ is formally smooth over $\Lambda$ if and only if all $q_i$ are non-degenerate.}
\item[(b)] \textit{$I_R(\mathfrak{X}|R) = \prod_{i=1}^r I_R(\mathfrak{X}_{z_i}|R)$ where each factor is a principal ideal.}
\end{itemize}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} (a) follows from the local computation in 6.2 and the global-to-local exact sequence. (b) follows from 5.7 and the fact that the singularities are isolated.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Theorem 6.5.} \textit{Let $X_0$ be a $k$-scheme of finite type with isolated non-smooth points only. Let $(R, \mathfrak{X})$ be a formal versal deformation of $X_0$. Assume that $\operatorname{char} k \neq 2$, $\dim X_0$ is even, and all non-smooth points are ordinary quadratic singularities. Then}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] \textit{$R$ is formally smooth over $\Lambda$.}
\item[(2)] \textit{$I_R(\mathfrak{X}|R)$ is a principal ideal and in turn so is $T_R(\mathfrak{X}|R) = \prod_{i=1}^r I_R(\mathfrak{X}_{z_i}|R)$. Furthermore, $I_R(\mathfrak{X}_{z_i}|R) = (t_i)$ and $t_1, \ldots, t_r$ can be extended to a system of free formal generators of $R$ over $\Lambda$.}
\item[(3)] \textit{$\mathfrak{X} \to \Spec(\Lambda)$ is formally smooth.}
\item[(4)] \textit{If $k$ is algebraically closed and $X_0$ is a complete irreducible curve, then $\dim R = 3g - 3 + a$ where $g = \dim_k H^1(X_0, \mathcal{O}_{X_0})$ and $a = \dim_k H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)$.}
\end{itemize}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Proof:} The statement follows by combining 6.2, 6.4, and the computation of dimensions via the exact sequence of Lemma 4.4 and Riemann-Roch for curves.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Corollary 6.6.} \textit{Under the assumptions of 6.5, the universal deformation of a curve with $r$ ordinary double points is obtained by smoothing each double point independently. The parameter space is smooth of dimension $3g - 3 + r$.}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{6.7.} We now consider the case of an ordinary quadratic singularity more explicitly. Let $A_0$ be a local $k$-algebra with $\widehat{A}_0 \simeq k[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]/(f)$ where $f$ is a non-degenerate quadratic form. Then the versal deformation is given by $R = \Lambda[[t]]$ and $\widehat{A} = R[[x_1, \ldots, x_n]]/(F - t)$ where $F$ lifts $f$. The singular locus of $\mathfrak{X}$ over $R$ is defined by the vanishing of the $n \times n$ minors of the Jacobian matrix, which reduces to the single equation $t = 0$ since $f$ is non-degenerate.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Corollary 6.8.} \textit{Let $X_0$ be a $k$-scheme of finite type with isolated non-smooth points only such that $H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^0) = 0$, and let $(R, \mathfrak{X})$ be a formal versal deformation of $X_0$. Denote by $\{z_1, \ldots, z_r\}$ the set of non-smooth points in $X_0$, and assume that $\operatorname{char} k \neq 2$, $\dim X_0$ is even, and all $z_i$'s are ordinary quadratic singularities. Then}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] \textit{$R$ is formally smooth over $\Lambda$.}
\item[(2)] \textit{$T_R(\mathfrak{X}|R) = \prod_{i=1}^r I_R(\mathfrak{X}_{z_i}|R)$ is a principal ideal. Furthermore, $I_R(\mathfrak{X}_{z_i}|R) = (t_i)$ and $t_1, \ldots, t_r$ can be extended to a system of free formal generators of $R$ over $\Lambda$.}
\item[(3)] \textit{$\mathfrak{X} \to \Spec(\Lambda)$ is formally smooth.}
\item[(4)] \textit{If $k$ is algebraically closed and $X_0$ is a complete irreducible curve, then $\dim R = 3g - 3 + a$ where $g = \dim_k H^1(X_0, \mathcal{O}_{X_0})$ and $a = \dim_k H^0(X_0, \underline{D}_{X_0}^1)$.}
\end{itemize}

\clearpage
\section*{SGA 7-I, Expose VII -- Biextensions of Sheaves of Groups}

\begin{center}
\textit{A. Grothendieck}
\end{center}

\subsection*{0. Introduction}

The notion of a \emph{biextension} was introduced by D. Mumford in the context of formal groups, in order to express conveniently the relations between the formal groups associated with an abelian scheme and with the dual abelian scheme. In the present expose we make a systematic study of this notion in the general context of sheaves of groups on a given site. In particular, we show that the notion fits in a remarkably simple way into the classical cohomological formalism of $\underline{\otimes}^{L}$ and $\underline{R\!\Hom}$.

In the following expose we shall spell out the most interesting special cases, especially the case of biextensions of group schemes by the multiplicative group $\underline{\mathbb G}_m$, working with sites such as the Zariski site, the fpqc site, or an intermediate site. One will see, in particular, that if $A$ and $B$ are abelian schemes over a scheme $S$, the biextensions of $(A,B)$ by $\underline{\mathbb G}_m$ have a remarkable interpretation: essentially, they are the classical divisorial correspondences on $A$ and $B$. Thus the cohomological meaning of the notion of divisorial correspondence, already implicit in Tate duality for abelian varieties over $p$-adic fields, is made explicit in a convenient form.

This interpretation of divisorial correspondences is also useful for following what becomes of such a correspondence when $A$ and $B$, supposed defined over the fraction field of a discrete valuation ring, are allowed to degenerate, using Neron theory. The results of Expose VIII will show, for example, that one obtains a biextension of the pair of identity components of the Neron models, and hence a biextension of the connected special fibres of the Neron models, playing the role of a specialization of the original divisorial correspondence. These results will be used in Expose IX, together with the monodromy theorem, to prove results on Neron models of abelian varieties, the most important being the semistable reduction theorem for the Neron model.

Throughout the sequel, unless explicitly stated otherwise, we work in a fixed $U$-topos, denoted $\mathcal T$. Thus $\mathcal T$ is a category equivalent to the category of sheaves on a suitable $U$-site, which may be taken to be $\mathcal T$ itself with its canonical topology. We shall often identify, terminologically, the objects of $\mathcal T$ with sheaves of sets on $\mathcal T$. The groups, extensions of groups, and so on that occur below are all relative to this topos $\mathcal T$.

\subsection*{1. Complements on extensions of groups}

\paragraph{1.1.} Let $P$ and $G$ be two groups of $\mathcal T$, and let
\begin{equation}
1 \longrightarrow G \xrightarrow{i} E \xrightarrow{j} P \longrightarrow 1
\tag{1.1.1}
\end{equation}
be an extension of $P$ by $G$. Thus $E$ is a group, $j:E\to P$ is an epimorphism of groups, and $i$ identifies $G$ with $\Ker j$. We shall reinterpret the datum of such an extension in terms which will be useful in the definition and study of biextensions.

\paragraph{1.1.1.} The datum of an epimorphism $j:E\to P$ is the datum of a torsor over $P$, with structural morphism $j$. The fact that $E$ is endowed with a group structure and that $j$ is a homomorphism of groups is equivalent to saying that the torsor $E$ is a group object in the topos of torsors over $P$, or equivalently in the topos $\mathcal T_P$ of objects of $\mathcal T$ endowed with an action of $P$. The datum of a homomorphism $i:G\to E$ making (1.1.1) exact is then the datum of a rigidification of this torsor by $G$, namely an isomorphism of $G$ with the subgroup $\Ker j$ of $E$. Thus an extension of $P$ by $G$ may be regarded as a torsor over $P$ rigidified by $G$.

\paragraph{1.1.2.} The homomorphism $i$ lets $G$ act on $E$ in two ways, on the left and on the right, by
\[
g\cdot x\cdot g'=i(g)\,x\,i(g')
\]
for sections $g,g'\in G(S)$ and $x\in E(S)$. If $E$ is viewed, via $j$, as an object over $P$, then this is the same as saying that the group induced by $G$ on $\mathcal T_{/P}$, namely $G_P=G\times P$, acts on $E$ on the left and on the right. Each of the two actions makes $E$ a torsor over $P$, with group $G_P$, and more precisely $E$ is a bitorsor under $(G_P,G_P)$. This means that $E$ is a right torsor for the right action of $G_P$, and the left action identifies $G_P$ with the sheaf of automorphisms of this right torsor; equivalently, the left action makes $E$ a left torsor and the right action identifies the opposite group $G_P^0$ with the automorphism group of the left torsor.

When $\mathcal T$ is the punctual topos, so that $P$ and $G$ are ordinary groups, a bitorsor under $(G_P,G_P)$ is the same as, for each $p\in P$, a bitorsor $E_p$ under $(G,G)$, namely the fibre of $E$ over $p$. The same interpretation holds in the general topos if one says it after every base change $S\to e$: for every point $p\in P(S)$ one obtains a bitorsor $E_p$ under $(G,G)$, these bitorsors being compatible with changes of base.

\paragraph{1.2.} Let $e_P$ be the unit section of $P$. The fibre $E_{e_P}$ of $E$ over $e_P$ is canonically isomorphic to $G$, and this is an isomorphism of bitorsors under $(G,G)$ when $G$ is given its usual bitorsor structure by left and right translations. A bitorsor $E$ under $(G_P,G_P)$ is said to be \emph{rigidified}, relative to $e_P$, when one is given an isomorphism of bitorsors
\[
G\xrightarrow{\sim}E_{e_P},
\]
or equivalently a section of this torsor.

The terminology is motivated by the case in which $G$ is commutative and
\begin{equation}
\Hom_{\mathrm{pt}}(P,G)\simeq (e),
\tag{1.3.1.1}
\end{equation}
where the left hand side denotes morphisms carrying $e_P$ to the unit section $e_G$ of $G$. Equivalently, the natural homomorphism
\begin{equation}
\Gamma(G)\xrightarrow{\sim}\Hom(P,G)
\tag{1.3.1.2}
\end{equation}
which assigns to a section $g$ of $G$ the constant morphism $P\to G$ with value $g$, is an isomorphism. These equivalent relations say that every automorphism of a torsor respecting the given rigidification is the identity. Under condition (1.3.1.1), the category of torsors under $G_P$ rigidified relative to $e_P$ is therefore rigid.

\paragraph{1.3.2.} Let $Q$ be an object of $\mathcal T$ endowed with a section $e_Q$, and let $E$ be a torsor under $G_{P\times Q}$. Since $e_Q$ defines a section of $P\times Q$ over $P$, one can consider rigidifications of $E$ relative to this section in the induced topos $\mathcal T_{/P}$; explicitly, these are sections of the torsor induced by $E$ on $P\times e_Q$. Interchanging the roles of $P$ and $Q$, one obtains partial rigidifications relative to $e_P\times Q$. Two such partial rigidifications are said to be compatible if their restrictions to $e_P\times e_Q$ coincide, that is, if they define the same section of the torsor which is the inverse image of $E$ by the section $(e_P,e_Q)$ of $P\times Q$.

A pair of compatible partial rigidifications is also called a \emph{birigidification} of the torsor $E$ relative to the pair of sections $(e_P,e_Q)$. If $G$ is commutative, $E$ admits a birigidification if and only if it admits the two partial rigidifications. Moreover one may prescribe either one of them in advance: one corrects the other by a morphism $Q\to G$ obtained by composing $Q\to e$ with the appropriate section of $G$.

Still in the commutative case, if $E$ and $E'$ are two birigidified torsors under $G_{P\times Q}$, then to say that they are isomorphic as plain $G_{P\times Q}$-torsors is equivalent to saying that they are isomorphic as birigidified torsors. Indeed, if $u:E\to E'$ is a torsor isomorphism, the discrepancy between the two birigidifications is measured by morphisms $f:P\to G$ and $g:Q\to G$ whose values at the distinguished sections have the same product $a\in G(e)$. The morphism
\[
 h:P\times Q\longrightarrow G,\qquad (p,q)\longmapsto f(p)a g(q)a^{-1}
\]
then corrects $u$ to an isomorphism respecting the birigidifications.

\paragraph{1.3.3.} The terminology just used extends immediately to the case where one has a bitorsor $E$ under $(G_P,G_Q)$. A rigidification of such a bitorsor is an isomorphism of the pull-back of $E$ by the marked section with the trivial bitorsor. Equivalently, it is given by a central section, i.e. by a section commuting with the relevant action. The terminology of birigidifications is extended in the same way to bitorsors.

\paragraph{1.3.4.} Return to the case of two groups $\mathcal P$ and $G$, and choose the unit section $e_{\mathcal P}$ in order to define rigidifications of bitorsors over $\mathcal P$, and birigidifications of bitorsors over $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$. There are natural functors
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{EXT}(\mathcal P;G)\xrightarrow{i}
\mathbf{BIEXTORS}_{\mathcal P}(\mathcal P;G)\xrightarrow{\delta}
\mathbf{BIEXTORS}_{\mathcal P\times\mathcal P}(\mathcal P;G).
\tag{1.3.4.1}
\end{equation}
Here the three terms denote respectively the category of extensions of groups of $\mathcal P$ by $G$, the category of bitorsors under $(G_{\mathcal P},G_{\mathcal P})$ rigidified at $e_{\mathcal P}$, and the category of bitorsors under $(G_{\mathcal P\times\mathcal P},G_{\mathcal P\times\mathcal P})$ birigidified at $(e_{\mathcal P},e_{\mathcal P})$.

The functor $i$ sends an extension $E$ of $\mathcal P$ by $G$ to its corresponding bitorsor over $\mathcal P$, rigidified by the unit section of $E$. The functor $\delta$ is defined by
\begin{equation}
\delta(E)=\pi^*(E)\cdot \mathrm{pr}_1^*(E)^{-1}\cdot \mathrm{pr}_2^*(E)^{-1},
\tag{1.3.4.2}
\end{equation}
where $\pi:\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\to\mathcal P$ is the group law and $\mathrm{pr}_1,\mathrm{pr}_2$ are the two projections. The bitorsor $\delta(E)$ has a canonical birigidification. For instance, its restriction to $\mathcal P\times e_{\mathcal P}$ is canonically the trivial bitorsor, using the given rigidification of $E$; the other partial rigidification is obtained symmetrically, and the two are plainly compatible.

The composite $\delta i$ is canonically isomorphic to the trivial functor, i.e. to the constant functor with value the trivial birigidified bitorsor over $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$. Indeed, for every extension $E$ of $\mathcal P$ by $G$, the usual multiplication isomorphism defining the extension gives a trivialization of $\delta i(E)$ compatible with its canonical birigidification.

\begin{proposition}
Let $\mathcal P$ be a group of $\mathcal T$.

\textup{(a)} Suppose that every morphism of sheaves of sets from $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$ to $G$ which is trivial on $\mathcal P\times e_{\mathcal P}$ and on $e_{\mathcal P}\times\mathcal P$ is trivial. Then the functor $i$ in \textup{(1.3.4.1)} is fully faithful. In particular, on a bitorsor $E$ under $(G_{\mathcal P},G_{\mathcal P})$, rigidified relative to $e_{\mathcal P}$, there is at most one structure of extension of $\mathcal P$ by $G$ compatible with its bitorsor structure and having $e_{\mathcal P}$ as unit section.

\textup{(b)} Suppose moreover that every morphism of sheaves of sets from $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$ to $G$ which is trivial on the three partial products
\[
\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\times e_{\mathcal P},\quad
\mathcal P\times e_{\mathcal P}\times\mathcal P,\quad
 e_{\mathcal P}\times\mathcal P\times\mathcal P
\]
is trivial. Let $E$ be a torsor under $G_{\mathcal P}$. There exists on $E$ a structure of extension of $\mathcal P$ by $G$, compatible with its bitorsor structure, if and only if the underlying bitorsor of
\[
\delta(E)=\pi^*(E)\cdot \mathrm{pr}_1^*(E)^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{pr}_2^*(E)^{-1}
\]
over $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$ is trivial. If this condition holds, then for every central section of $E$ over $e_{\mathcal P}$, i.e. for every rigidification of $E$ relative to $e_{\mathcal P}$, there is a unique extension structure on $E$ compatible with the bitorsor structure and with the prescribed unit section. Equivalently, $E$, as a rigidified bitorsor, lies in the essential image of $i$. In addition, the birigidified bitorsor $\delta(E)$ has a unique trivialization.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For (a), let $E$ and $E'$ be two extensions of $\mathcal P$ by $G$, and let $f:E\to E'$ be a morphism of the underlying rigidified bitorsors. We must prove that $f$ is a morphism of extensions, equivalently a group homomorphism. It is enough to prove $f(xy)=f(x)f(y)$. If $j':E'\to\mathcal P$ is the structural morphism, then $j'f(xy)=j'(f(x)f(y))$, because both sides have the same image in $\mathcal P$. Hence
\[
f(xy)=u(jx,jy)\,f(x)f(y)
\]
for a well-defined morphism $u:\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\to G$. Taking successively $x=1$ and $y=1$, and using $f(1)=1$, gives
\[
u(p,1)=u(1,p)=1.
\]
Thus $u$ is trivial on $\mathcal P\times e_{\mathcal P}$ and on $e_{\mathcal P}\times\mathcal P$, and by hypothesis $u$ is trivial. Therefore $f(xy)=f(x)f(y)$.

For (b), the necessity is immediate from the preceding construction of $\delta$. Conversely, choose a trivialization $\Phi$ of $\delta(E)$. Pulling it back by the unit section of $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$ gives a rigidification of $E$ relative to $e_{\mathcal P}$; fix this rigidification. The trivialization $\Phi$ need not be compatible with the induced birigidification of $\delta(E)$. Its discrepancies along $\mathcal P\times e_{\mathcal P}$ and $e_{\mathcal P}\times\mathcal P$ are given by central morphisms
\[
f:\mathcal P\to G,\qquad g:\mathcal P\to G
\]
with the same value $a\in G(e)$ at the unit. The central morphism
\[
h:\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\to G,\qquad (p,q)\longmapsto f(p)g(q)a^{-1}
\]
has these two restrictions. Replacing $\Phi$ by $\Phi(p,q)h(p,q)^{-1}$, we get a trivialization of $\delta(E)$ compatible with its birigidification. Such a trivialization is unique, since any two differ by a central morphism $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\to G$ trivial on the two partial products, hence trivial.

It remains to check associativity of the multiplication defined by $\Phi$, knowing already that $e_{\mathcal P}$ is a two-sided unit. The two composites in the associativity condition differ by a central morphism
\[
u:\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\to G
\]
through the formula
\[
(xy)z=u(jx,jy,jz)\,x(yz).
\]
Putting successively $y=z=1$, then $z=x=1$, then $x=y=1$, and using the fact that $1$ is a two-sided unit, one sees that $u$ is trivial on the three partial products. By hypothesis it is trivial, and hence $(xy)z=x(yz)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Suppose that every morphism of sheaves of sets
\[
\mathcal P\times G\longrightarrow G
\]
which is trivial on $\mathcal P\times e_{\mathcal P}$ and on $e_{\mathcal P}\times G$ is trivial. Then, if $G$ is commutative, $G$ is central in every extension of groups of $\mathcal P$ by $G$. If $\mathcal P$ is also commutative and the hypothesis of part \textup{(a)} of the proposition holds, then every extension $E$ of $\mathcal P$ by $G$ is commutative.
\end{corollary}

Indeed, to prove the first assertion one must show that the action of $\mathcal P$ on $G$ defined by the extension is trivial. This action is represented by a morphism $u:\mathcal P\times G\to G$ whose restrictions to $\mathcal P\times e_{\mathcal P}$ and $e_{\mathcal P}\times G$ are trivial in the required sense; the hypothesis gives the assertion. For the second assertion, note that the opposite group $E^0$ of an extension $E$ of $\mathcal P$ by $G$ is an extension of $\mathcal P^0$ by $G^0$, and the underlying bitorsor of $i(E^0)$ is obtained from $i(E)$ by exchanging left and right operations. If $E$ is central, then $i(E)=i(E^0)$; if in addition $\mathcal P$ is commutative, uniqueness in the proposition gives $E=E^0$, i.e. $E$ is commutative.

\paragraph{1.3.7.} A particularly useful case in which all the preceding hypotheses are satisfied is the case where
\begin{equation}
\underline{\Hom}_{\bullet}(\mathcal P,G)=e
\tag{1.3.7.1}
\end{equation}
where the left hand side denotes the sheaf of morphisms of pointed sheaves of sets from $\mathcal P$ to $G$. Independently of the marked sections, this condition may also be written in the form
\begin{equation}
G=\underline{\Hom}(\mathcal P,G),
\tag{1.3.7.2}
\end{equation}
where the right hand side is the sheaf of morphisms of underlying sheaves of sets. It also says that, for every object $\mathcal Q$ of $\mathcal T$, the map
\begin{equation}
u\longmapsto u\circ\mathrm{pr}_1:
\underline{\Hom}_{\bullet}(\mathcal P,G)
\longrightarrow
\underline{\Hom}_{\bullet}(\mathcal P\times\mathcal Q,G)
\tag{1.3.7.3}
\end{equation}
is bijective.

This property of $\mathcal P$, for fixed $G$, is stable under cartesian products. It therefore implies the same property for $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$, for $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$, and so on. Consequently it implies the hypotheses of the proposition and of the corollary. Moreover the categories occurring in (1.3.4.1) are all rigid, meaning that every automorphism of an object in one of these categories is the identity. Since condition (1.3.7.1) is stable under every base change, descent and uniqueness show that a rigidified bitorsor $E$ over $\mathcal P$ corresponds to an extension of $\mathcal P$ by $G$ as soon as this is true locally; equivalently, by the proposition, this is expressed by the vanishing of the section
\[
\delta(E)\in \mathbf H^0(G_{\mathcal P})
\]
defined by $\delta(E)$.

\paragraph{1.3.8.} Let $p:\mathcal P\to S$ be a morphism of schemes, quasi-compact and quasi-separated and flat, such that
\begin{equation}
p_*\mathcal O_{\mathcal P}\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathcal O_S.
\tag{1.3.8.1}
\end{equation}
Using the fact that, by coherence of $p$, the formation of $p_*\mathcal O_{\mathcal P}$ commutes with flat base change, one sees immediately that these conditions on the relative scheme $\mathcal P/S$ are stable under cartesian products, in particular under the powers $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$, $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$, and so forth. They are also stable under every flat base change.

If $G$ is an affine scheme over $S$, condition (1.3.7.2) follows at once from (1.3.8.1), since, writing $G=\Spec(A)$,
\[
\underline{\Hom}_S(\mathcal P,G)=\Hom_S(A,p_*\mathcal O_{\mathcal P})=\Hom_S(A,\mathcal O_S).
\]
The same conclusion applies to the cartesian powers of $\mathcal P$, and, if $G$ is flat over $S$, to the relative scheme $\mathcal P_G$ over $G$ with respect to $G_G$.

When $\mathcal P$ and $G$ are group schemes over $S$, and one works with a topology on $(\mathrm{Sch})_{/S}$ at least as fine as the canonical topology, so that $\mathcal P$ and $G$ may be identified with sheaves on the site, every morphism from $\mathcal P$, $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$, $\mathcal P\times\mathcal P\times\mathcal P$, and so on to $G$ which is compatible with the marked sections is trivial. If $G$ is flat, the same is true for every $S$-morphism $\mathcal P\times G\to G$ compatible with the marked sections, by interpreting it as a $G$-morphism $\mathcal P_G\to G_G$. Thus the hypotheses above apply, and the categories in (1.3.4.1) are rigid.

The most important case in which (1.3.8.1) holds, and remains true after every extension of the base, is that in which $\mathcal P$ is an abelian scheme over $S$. In this case the preceding conclusions hold for every affine group $G$ over $S$. This is the case initially considered by J.-P. Serre, from which the preceding developments are visibly inspired. A case in which (1.3.8.1) holds without being universally true is when $\mathcal P$ is the Neron model of an abelian scheme over the fraction field of a discrete valuation ring with spectrum $S$.

\subsection*{1.4. Extensions of a free commutative group}

Let $I$ be an object of $\mathcal T$, and put
\begin{equation}
\mathcal P=\mathbb Z^{(I)},
\tag{1.4.1}
\end{equation}
the free $\mathbb Z$-module generated by $I$. To an extension $E$ of $\mathcal P$ by a given $\mathbb Z$-module $G$ -- in what follows only commutative extensions of groups will be considered -- associate the corresponding torsor under $G_{\mathcal P}$, and then restrict it to $I$ by the canonical morphism
\[
I\longrightarrow \mathcal P=\mathbb Z^{(I)}.
\]
This gives a canonical functor
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{EXT}(\mathcal P,G)\longrightarrow \mathbf{TORS}(I,G_I).
\tag{1.4.2}
\end{equation}
This functor is an equivalence of categories. Equivalently, for every $G$ the maps
\begin{equation}
\Hom_{\mathbb Z}(\mathcal P,G)\longrightarrow \mathrm H^0(I,G_I)=G(I)
\tag{1.4.3}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\Ext^1_{\mathbb Z}(\mathcal P,G)\longrightarrow \mathrm H^1(I,G_I)
\tag{1.4.4}
\end{equation}
are isomorphisms.

More generally, if $A$ is a not necessarily commutative ring of $\mathcal T$ and $\mathcal P=A^{(I)}$ is the free $A$-module generated by $I$, then there are canonical isomorphisms, functorial in the variable $A$-module $G$,
\begin{equation}
\Ext_A^i(A^{(I)},G)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm H^i(I,G),\qquad i\ge0,
\tag{1.4.5}
\end{equation}
which for $A=\mathbb Z$ and $i=0,1$ coincide with (1.4.3) and (1.4.4). To define (1.4.5), recall that the left hand side is the derived functor of
\[
G\longmapsto \Hom_A(A^{(I)},G),
\]
which by the definition of $A^{(I)}$ is the functor $G\mapsto G(I)=\mathrm H^0(I,G)$. Since $G\mapsto G_I$ sends injective modules to injective modules in $\mathcal T_{/I}$, the desired conclusion follows. The announced compatibility with (1.4.3) and (1.4.4) is left to the reader.

The functor (1.4.2) is compatible with inverse images by a morphism of topoi
\[
f:\mathcal T'\longrightarrow\mathcal T.
\]
In rough terms, this is the commutativity of the diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
\mathbf{EXT}(\mathcal P,G)&\longrightarrow&\mathbf{TORS}(I,G_I)\\
\downarrow f^*&&\downarrow f^*\\
\mathbf{EXT}(f^*\mathcal P,f^*G)&\longrightarrow&\mathbf{TORS}(f^*I,(f^*G)_{f^*I})
\end{array}
\]
up to the canonical transitivity isomorphisms
\[
f^*(G_I)\xrightarrow{\sim}(f^*G)_{f^*I},\qquad
f^*(\mathbb Z^{(I)})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb Z^{(f^*I)}.
\]
We leave to the reader the precise formulation of these compatibilities.

\section*{2. The notion of a biextension of abelian sheaves}

\paragraph{2.0.} Throughout the present section, $P$, $Q$, and $G$ denote commutative groups of the topos $\mathcal T$. We shall often use the cartesian diagram
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{ccc}
P\times Q&\xrightarrow{\mathrm{pr}_1}&P\\
\downarrow{\scriptstyle \mathrm{pr}_2}&&\downarrow\\
Q&\longrightarrow&e.
\end{array}
\tag{2.0.1}
\end{equation}
We denote by $G_P$, $G_Q$, and $G_{P\times Q}$ the inverse images of the group $G$ on $e$ on $P$, on $Q$, and on $P\times Q$ respectively. We identify $G_{P\times Q}$ indifferently with the inverse image $(G_P)_{P\times Q}$ of $G_P$ by $\mathrm{pr}_1$ and with the inverse image $(G_Q)_{P\times Q}$ of $G_Q$ by $\mathrm{pr}_2$. In these notations the group structures of $P$, $Q$, and $P\times Q$ do not intervene. In fact, we shall practically never need to use the product group structure on $P\times Q$. On the other hand, it will be useful to consider on $P\times Q$ the structure of a $P$-group obtained by inverse image from the group $Q$ on $e$,
\begin{equation}
P\times Q=Q_P\longrightarrow P,
\tag{2.0.2}
\end{equation}
and similarly the structure of a $Q$-group obtained by inverse image from the group $P$ on $e$,
\begin{equation}
P\times Q=P_Q\longrightarrow Q.
\tag{2.0.3}
\end{equation}
Thus on $P$ we shall have to consider the two groups $G_P$ and $Q_P$, and on $Q$ the two groups $G_Q$ and $P_Q$.

Let $E$ be a torsor on $P\times Q$ under the group $G_{P\times Q}$. It may be regarded indifferently as a torsor on $Q_P$ under the group $G_{P\times Q}=(G_Q)_P$, or as a torsor on $P_Q$ under the group $G_{P\times Q}=(G_P)_Q$. It therefore makes sense to consider on $E$ a structure of commutative extension of $Q_P$ by $G_P$ compatible with its torsor structure, or, symmetrically, a structure of extension of $P_Q$ by $G_Q$ compatible with its torsor structure. Moreover, applying the preceding discussion on extensions to the induced topoi $\mathcal T_{/P}$ and $\mathcal T_{/Q}$ gives a convenient way to make such structures explicit. Using the canonical isomorphisms
\[
\mathcal T_{/P\times Q}\simeq (\mathcal T_{/P})_{/P\times Q},\qquad
\mathcal T_{/P\times Q}\simeq (\mathcal T_{/Q})_{/P\times Q},
\]
one obtains the following descriptions.

A structure of extension of $Q_P$ by $G_P$ on the torsor $E$ is equivalent to giving, for every object $S$ of $\mathcal T$ and elements $p,p'\in P(S)$, $q\in Q(S)$, an isomorphism of torsors under $G$,
\begin{equation}
\rho_{p,p';q}:E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p',q}\xrightarrow{\sim}E_{pp',q},
\tag{2.0.4}
\end{equation}
satisfying the conditions recalled below. As usual, for a point $(p,q)\in (P\times Q)(S)=P(S)\times Q(S)$, the notation $E_{p,q}$ denotes the torsor under $G$ obtained by inverse image of the torsor $E$ under $G_{P\times Q}$ by the morphism $(p,q):S\to P\times Q$. The data (2.0.4) are required to be functorial in $S$, that is, compatible with inverse images by morphisms $S'\to S$ in $\mathcal T$, and to satisfy associativity, expressed by the commutativity of diagrams of the form
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{ccc}
E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p',q}\cdot E_{p'',q}&\longrightarrow&E_{pp',q}\cdot E_{p'',q}\\
\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p'p'',q}&\longrightarrow&E_{pp'p'',q},
\end{array}
\tag{2.0.5}
\end{equation}
where $p,p',p''\in P(S)$ and $q\in Q(S)$. They must also satisfy commutativity, expressed by the commutativity of the diagrams
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{ccc}
E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p',q}&\xrightarrow{\rho}&E_{pp',q}\\
\downarrow{\scriptstyle \mathrm{sym}}&&\uparrow\\
E_{p',q}\cdot E_{p,q}&\longrightarrow&E_{p'p,q},
\end{array}
\tag{2.0.6}
\end{equation}
where $p,p'\in P(S)$ and $q\in Q(S)$.

One similarly makes explicit a structure of extension of $P_Q$ by $G_Q$ on $E$ by means of isomorphisms of torsors under $G$
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{p;q,q'}:E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p,q'}\xrightarrow{\sim}E_{p,qq'}
\tag{2.0.7}
\end{equation}
for $p\in P(S)$ and $q,q'\in Q(S)$. The $\gamma$ are functorial in $S$ and satisfy the associativity and commutativity conditions symmetric to (2.0.5) and (2.0.6), expressed by the commutativity of the diagrams
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{ccc}
E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p,q'}\cdot E_{p,q''}&\longrightarrow&E_{p,qq'}\cdot E_{p,q''}\\
\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p,q'q''}&\longrightarrow&E_{p,qq'q''},
\end{array}
\tag{2.0.8}
\end{equation}
where $p\in P(S)$ and $q,q',q''\in Q(S)$, and
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{ccc}
E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p,q'}&\xrightarrow{\gamma}&E_{p,qq'}\\
\downarrow{\scriptstyle \mathrm{sym}}&&\uparrow\\
E_{p,q'}\cdot E_{p,q}&\longrightarrow&E_{p,q'q}.
\end{array}
\tag{2.0.9}
\end{equation}

\subsection*{2.1. Definition of the notion of biextension}

\begin{definition}
Let $P$, $Q$, and $G$ be abelian groups of $\mathcal T$, and let $E$ be a torsor under $G_{P\times Q}$. A \emph{biextension of $(P,Q)$ by $G$} is such a torsor $E$, endowed with a structure of extension of $Q_P$ by $G_P$ defined by isomorphisms (2.0.4), functorial in $S$ and subject to the conditions expressing the commutativity of (2.0.5) and (2.0.6), and with a structure of extension of $P_Q$ by $G_Q$ defined by isomorphisms (2.0.7), functorial in $S$ and subject to the conditions expressed by the commutativity of (2.0.8) and (2.0.9). These two structures are said to be \emph{compatible} if, for every object $S$ of $\mathcal T$ and for $p,p'\in P(S)$, $q,q'\in Q(S)$, the following diagram is commutative:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{ccc}
E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p,q'}\cdot E_{p',q}\cdot E_{p',q'}&\longrightarrow&E_{p,qq'}\cdot E_{p',qq'}\\
\downarrow&&\downarrow{\scriptstyle \rho}\\
E_{pp',q}\cdot E_{pp',q'}&\xrightarrow{\gamma}&E_{pp',qq'}.
\end{array}
\tag{2.1.1}
\end{equation}
Here one of the vertical arrows is deduced from $\rho$ and $\gamma$ by symmetry, and the other uses the symmetry isomorphism
\[
E_{p,q}\cdot E_{p',q'}\xrightarrow{\sim}E_{p',q'}\cdot E_{p,q}.
\]
\end{definition}

\paragraph{2.1.1.} Equivalently, a biextension structure on the $G_{P\times Q}$-torsor $E$ is defined by two partially defined laws of composition $\oplus(x,y)$ and $\otimes(x,y)$ on $E$. The first is defined when $\mathrm{pr}_1 j(x)=\mathrm{pr}_1 j(y)$, and the second when $\mathrm{pr}_2 j(x)=\mathrm{pr}_2 j(y)$, where $j:E\to P\times Q$ is the structural morphism of the torsor. In addition to the usual compatibilities with the action of $G$, such as
\begin{equation}
g\oplus(x,y)=\oplus(x,gy)=\oplus(gx,y)
\quad (x,y\in E(S),\ g\in G(S)),
\tag{2.1.1.1}
\end{equation}
and the analogous relations for $\otimes$, the axioms are the associativity and commutativity of these partial laws of composition, together with the relation
\begin{equation}
\otimes(\oplus(x,y),\oplus(z,t))=
\oplus(\otimes(x,z),\otimes(y,t))
\tag{2.1.1.2}
\end{equation}
for $x,y,z,t$ respectively in $E_{p,q}(S)$, $E_{p,q'}(S)$, $E_{p',q}(S)$, and $E_{p',q'}(S)$.

There is also reason to consider the unit section of $E$ when $E$ is regarded as an extension of $Q_P$ by $G_P$,
\begin{equation}
e_{P/E}\in \Gamma(E/P)=\Hom_P(P,E),
\tag{2.2.1}
\end{equation}
and, symmetrically, the unit section of $E$ when it is regarded as an extension of $P_Q$ by $G_Q$,
\begin{equation}
e_{Q/E}\in \Gamma(E/Q)=\Hom_Q(Q,E).
\tag{2.2.2}
\end{equation}
In particular, the torsor $E$ over $P\times Q$, with group $G_{P\times Q}$, is canonically trivialized above $1_P\times Q$ by $e_{P/E}$, and above $P\times 1_Q$ by $e_{Q/E}$. These two trivializations moreover coincide above the unit section $1_P\times 1_Q$ of $P\times Q$, and therefore define a single section
\begin{equation}
e\in \Gamma(E/e)=\Hom_e(e,E),
\tag{2.2.3}
\end{equation}
which, by abuse of language, will be called the unit section, or neutral section, of $E$.

To prove the asserted equality of the two sections $e,e'$ of $E$ obtained respectively from $e_{P/E}$ and $e_{Q/E}$, note that they are characterized respectively as the unit sections in the inverse image $E|_e$ of the unit section of $P\times Q$ in $E$, for the two laws of composition $\oplus$ and $\otimes$. Applying (2.1.1.2) to the sections $e,e',e',e$ of $E|_e$, one finds that the two sides are respectively equal to $e$ and $e'$, whence $e=e'$. It also follows that the two composition laws $\oplus$ and $\otimes$ on $E|_e$ are identical. Indeed, it is immediate that they correspond to the group law of $G$ transported by means of the morphisms $g\mapsto g\cdot e$ and $g\mapsto g\cdot e'$ respectively; hence they agree since $e=e'$.

Finally, the extension $E$ of $Q_P$ by $G_P$ over the base $P$, when restricted above the section $1_P$ of $P$, gives a structure of extension of $Q$ by $G$ on the inverse image of $1_P\times Q$ in $E$. The homomorphism $e_{P/E}$ is immediately seen to be multiplicative for this extension structure; hence this extension is canonically split. Symmetrically, the extension of $P$ by $G$ obtained by restricting the extension over $Q$ above the section $1_Q$ is canonically split by $e_{Q/E}$.


\clearpage
\section*{SGA 7-I, Exposé VII.2.3 through Exposé IX.6.6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{SGA 7-I, Exposé VII.2.3 through Exposé IX.6.6}

\section*{Exposé VII. Biextensions of sheaves of groups, continued}

\subsection*{2.3. Morphisms of biextensions}

Let \(E\) be a biextension of \((P,Q)\) by \(G\), and likewise let \(E'\) be a
biextension of \((P',Q')\) by \(G'\), where \(P',Q'\), and \(G'\) are again
commutative groups of the topos \(\cT\). A \emph{morphism of biextensions}
from \(E\) to \(E'\) is a system
\[
(u,v,w,f)
\]
where
\[
u:P\longrightarrow P',\qquad v:Q\longrightarrow Q',
        \qquad w:G\longrightarrow G'
\]
are homomorphisms of groups, and where
\[
f:E\longrightarrow E'
\]
is a morphism of the underlying sheaves of sets, such that \(f\) is at the same
time a homomorphism of extensions over \(P\), respectively over \(P'\), associated
with the corresponding homomorphisms of relative groups, and a homomorphism of
extensions over \(Q\), respectively over \(Q'\), associated with the corresponding
homomorphisms of relative groups. In other words the two group-extension
structures carried by a biextension must be respected simultaneously.

The maps \(u,v,w\) are already determined by \(f\). Passing to the quotient
recovers \(u\times v\), hence \(u\) and \(v\) by restriction to
\(P\times 1_Q\) and \(1_P\times Q\). Restricting \(f\) to the copy of \(G\)
through the neutral section recovers \(w\). Thus no ambiguity results from
identifying the morphism \((u,v,w,f)\) with the single map \(f\).

With the usual precaution of recording source and target, biextensions in
\(\cT\) are therefore the objects of a category. We denote it by
\(\BIEXT(\cT)\). The assignment
\[
(E;P,Q,G)\longmapsto (P,Q,G)
\]
defines a canonical functor
\[
\BIEXT(\cT)\longrightarrow
\GRAB(\cT)\times\GRAB(\cT)\times\GRAB(\cT),
\]
where \(\GRAB(\cT)\) denotes the category of abelian groups in \(\cT\). It is
often useful to decompose this functor into the two functors
\[
E\longmapsto (P,Q),\qquad E\longmapsto G.
\]

\subsection*{2.4. The category \(\BIEXT(P,Q;G)\) with \(P,Q,G\) variable}

The first of the two preceding functors is fibred, and the second is cofibrant.
Concretely, if \(E\) is a biextension of \((P,Q)\) by \(G\), and if
\[
u:P_1\to P,\qquad v:Q_1\to Q
\]
are homomorphisms of abelian groups, then there is a biextension \(E'\) of
\((P_1,Q_1)\) by \(G\) and a morphism of biextensions
\[
E'\longrightarrow E
\]
compatible with \(u,v,\id_G\), having the usual universal property. This
biextension is the inverse image of \(E\) by \((u,v)\).

Dually, if \(w:G\to G'\) is a homomorphism of abelian groups, there is a
biextension \(E'\) of \((P,Q)\) by \(G'\) and a morphism of biextensions
\[
E\longrightarrow E'
\]
compatible with \(\id_P,\id_Q,w\), again characterized by its universal property.
It is the push-out of \(E\) by \(w\). These constructions are obtained by
paraphrasing the corresponding constructions for ordinary extensions of groups.

For fixed \(P,Q,G\) we write
\[
\BIEXT(P,Q;G)
\]
for the fibre category over the triple \((P,Q,G)\). The preceding description
gives the usual functorialities:
\[
(u,v)^*:\BIEXT(P,Q;G)\to \BIEXT(P_1,Q_1;G),
\]
and
\[
w_*:\BIEXT(P,Q;G)\to \BIEXT(P,Q;G').
\]
These are compatible in the evident sense.

\subsection*{2.5. The structure of \(\BIEXT(P,Q;G)\)}

For fixed \(P,Q,G\), the category \(\BIEXT(P,Q;G)\) is a strictly commutative
Picard category. The sum of two biextensions is defined by taking the contracted
product over \(P\times Q\):
\[
E+E'=(E\times_{P\times Q}E')/G,
\]
where \(G\) is embedded diagonally with the usual opposite action on one factor.
The two extension laws on \(E\) and \(E'\) induce the two required extension laws
on \(E+E'\), and the compatibility axiom for a biextension is inherited from the
corresponding axioms for \(E\) and \(E'\).

The neutral object is the trivial biextension
\[
P\times Q\times G
\]
with its evident partial addition laws. The inverse of a biextension is obtained
by replacing the \(G\)-torsor \(E\) by its opposite torsor. Associativity and
commutativity are inherited from the corresponding facts for contracted products
of torsors. Thus the isomorphism classes form an abelian group, denoted
\[
\Biext^1(P,Q;G).
\]

The automorphism group of the neutral biextension is also useful. An automorphism
of the trivial biextension over \(P\times Q\) is given by a morphism
\[
P\times Q\longrightarrow G.
\]
The condition that it respect both extension structures says precisely that this
morphism is additive separately in \(P\) and in \(Q\). Thus
\[
\operatorname{Aut}_{\BIEXT(P,Q;G)}(0)
        \simeq \operatorname{Bilin}(P,Q;G),
\]
the group of biadditive morphisms \(P\times Q\to G\). We denote this group by
\[
\Biext^0(P,Q;G).
\]

The two groups \(\Biext^0\) and \(\Biext^1\) behave functorially in all three
arguments. Pulling back in \(P\) and \(Q\), and pushing out in \(G\), gives the
corresponding homomorphisms of Picard categories and hence of their homotopy
groups.

\subsection*{2.6. Opposite and transposed biextensions}
